About the Town
SETTLED:
1718
INCORPORATED:
August 1, 1728
TYPE OF GOVERNMENT:
Town Meeting
LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by Townsend on
the north, Shirley on the east, Leominster and Lancaster
on the south, and Fitchburg and Ashby on the west. It
is about 47 miles from Boston and 29 miles from Worcester.
COUNTY:
Worcester
ELEVATION AT TOWN HALL:
Approximately 570 ft. above mean sea level

===PAGE 6===
Board of Selectmen
Board of Selectmen: left to right - Rudolph Masella, Chairman Normand J. Thompson and Sherwood G. Winchester.
In the year 1973 Lunenburg faced the "transitional year" -- the year when all communities in the Common-
wealth of Massachusetts converted from the calendar year to the July 1st fiscal year. All departments entered the
year with some trepidation, as they were concerned about accurately forecasting their budget needs for an eighteen
month period rather than the twelve month period to which they were well accustomed.
Each and every one of these departments should be commended as the only problems which have been encoun-
tered in regards to staying within budget appropriations were beyond the control or foresight of anyone in local gov-
ernment. Who could have predicted the energy crisis with any degree of accuracy? Who could have predicted that
the State House would do a "flip-flop" on its approach to highway allocations? Many area towns experienced fin-
ancial problems which required special town meetings to bring about solutions. Lunenburg recently had one which
will not affect our future tax rate.
The year 1973 was also the year of the energy crisis which required US to cut back as much as possible on all of
our energy usage. All Town Departments have worked hard to assure that they did not use more than was absolutely
necessary, and they have done an admirable job. The Board of Selectmen have decided to hold the addition of any
further street lighting to an absolute minimum due to the present energy crisis.
During 1973 over $20,389.00 was provided under the Federal Emergency Employment Act of 1971 for personnel
employed by the Town. For the period October 1971 through December 31, 1973, this has amounted to over $50,431.00
that the Town of Lunenburg has received for personnel employed under this Act.
-4-
===PAGE 7===
The Board of Selectmen acting in its various capacities held thirty-six regularly scheduled meetings throughout
the year and approved expenditures amounting to $6,602,333.52. As the Local Licensing Authority ten hearings were
held during the year. These hearings were held relative to applications submitted for renewal of licenses, and ap-
plications for issuance of initial licenses for the sale of various types of alcoholic beverages and also applications for
licenses to store inflammables.
The Highway Department, under the supervision of Superintendent George G. Munyon, Jr., continued to main-
tain our streets. Many new procedures have been instituted which will save money, and at the same time keep serv-
ices as they were or will improve them. Not the least of these changes is the usage of two-thirds less salt than was
previously used in our Snow Removal Program. This should have a decidedly good effect on our ecology.
The usage of our Rescue Unit continues to be of ever-increasing value to townspeople. Under the capable su-
pervision of Fire Chief, James P. Deming, there were 142 calls made during the year with 143 people being trans-
ported.
In 1973 the Town of Lunenburg as it has in previous years received the Massachusetts Selectmen's Association
Award for our 1972 Annual Town Report. This Report was compiled under the direction of Mr. Richard J. Eley with
the help of many other people all of whom are to be congratulated for their fine work.
The Board wishes to express its appreciation to the citizens of Lunenburg for their cooperation and understanding
throughout the year. We consider it a privilege and honor to serve as Selectmen in a Town such as Lunenburg.
Board of Assessors
The Board of Assessorshad a very active year in preparation for the new fiscal year cycle. All new and known
construction has been assessed so that the Town can collect all taxes that are due. There will be two tax bills in
1974--one for the six month period January 1, 1974 through June 30, 1974 and another for Fiscal Year 1975 which
is from July 1, 1974 through June 30, 1975. The latter bill will be payable in two installments.
The Assessors have again attempted to maintain the program established by revaluation in 1970. This was done
by continually reviewing properties, maintaining sales records and holding extra meetings. The Assessors have found
that the inflationary trend has most certainly affected the value of real estate. The increased cost of building supplies
has also affected this value.
New construction has increased the total valuation of Real Estate to $51,014,700 for 1973 as compared to
$49,847,000 for 1972. Personal Property valuations amounted to $2,712,180, an increase from the $2,180,490 in
1972. These increases give a total valuation for the Town of $53,726,880. In 1973 the tax rate was increased again
from $46.00 to $52.00 as a result of increased expenditures and the eighteen month budget. The Assessors hope that
the six month budget will show a reduction because of fixed expenditures voted at the 1973 Annual Town Meeting.
Mrs. Joan Souliere was hired to do the clerical work for this department to fill the vacancy created by the
resignation of Mrs. Donna LeCuyer. Board members again attended school as required by the Department of Corporations
& Taxation with the school being held this year at Fitchburg State College. The Board wishes to remind taxpayers
that certain exemptions have been enacted for the value of residential property, not exceeding specified amounts,
owned and occupied as a domicile by persons of various classes for whom the payment of taxes could constitute an
undue burden. If a taxpayer feels that they qualify for any of the following exemptions, they should contact the
Assessors' Office and request an application which lists all the requirements that must be met in order to qualify.
Section 17 - Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70 - $2,000 in valuation or a tax of $175,
whichever is greater.
Section 22 - Disabled or wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents - $2,000 to $10,000 or a tax from
$175 to $875, whichever is greater, depending on degree of disability.
Section 37 - Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Section 41 - Persons aged 70 or over - $4,000 valuation or a tax of $350, whichever is greater.
Section 42 - Unmarried widows of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valuation or a
tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Section 43 - Minor orphans of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 in valuation or a
tax of $700, whichever is greater.

===PAGE 9===
Park Commission
The highlights of the 1973 Park Commission activities were the expanded Tennis Program; the use of the Town
Beach facilities; Girls' Softball; and the new refreshment stand at the Marshall Park Little League Field.
Our Tennis Program, under the leadership of Dave Johnston, realized an all time high in attendance including
a large number of adults taking tennis instructions. Our Town Beach, under Jerry Beloin's guidance, was utilized
more this year than any year since the second year of operation. The addition of the new raft proved to be very popu-
lar with the residents. The hot summer, the new raft and the gasoline shortage had much to do in the greater utili-
zation of this facility. Our life saving and swimming programs under the able direction of June Riddle were a great
success again and assures US of continued qualified applicants for lifeguards for the beach. These programs are con-
ducted in conjunction with the Lunenburg Red Cross.
The Lunenburg Girls' Softball Team, under the tutilage of Chuck Winneberg, had a very successful season.
This program was a lost cause two years ago, but thanks to Chuck's enthusiasm and organization, Lunenburg enlisted
into a girls' league in the North County this summer. The future of this program appears bright. Thanks to our Lu-
nenburg Little League Organization, we now have a refreshment stand at the Marshall Park Little League Field.
This project required many free hours and money from many Lunenburg residents. The end resultwas a structure dedi-
cated in memory of Mrs. T. K. Ware which will be utilized as an officials' stand and will help defray expenses of
Lunenburg Little League Baseball.
The Park Commission supports many other programs which include Winter Tennis, Junior Basketball, Teen-Age
(non-team playing) Basketball, Young Men's Basketball, Open Gym for all residents, Adult Physical Education,
Skating at Marshall Pond (no lights this year), Junior Hockey, AAU Track Meets, Men's Golf, Couples' Scotch
Foursome Golf League, Men's Softball, Summer Teen-Age and Junior Basketball, Street Hockey (not successful),
Babe Ruth Baseball, Pop Warner Football, Summer Morning Baseball, Arts and Crafts, and playground activities which
included trips to Benson's Animal Farm, Willard Brook State Park and many side trips in town. Other Playground
activities included intra-playground competition, a picnic at Normand Park, swimming at the Town Beach and bar-
beques. The Playground Supervisors, in conjunction with the Arts & Crafts Supervisors, conducted another very suc-
cessful Muscular Dystrophy Fair this year.
This year for the first time Marshall Park was utilized by the Lunenburg High School Girls' Field Hockey Team.
Fitchburg State Baseball Team and the Fitchburg Center for Brain Damaged Children also utilized Marshall Park.
Many thanks to the taxpayers of Lunenburg as the Park Commission now has the storage space and the tools it needs
to get the job done without depending on other departments. We feel that this has saved you many tax dollars. Our
parks are basically in good repair under the capable supervision of Bob Garneau. Wallis Park has been repaired and
sealed, the Marshall Park driveway has been oiled, and the fence at Wallis Park has been repaired as has the backstop
at the Marshall Park Baseball Field.
Our plans for 1974 are to remove the condemned Quonset Hut at Wallis Park and erect a new building in its
place, paint the fence at Wallis Park and backstop at the Marshall Park Baseball Field and install cement slabs for
the Marshall Park Baseball Field. The Park Commission also plans to change the location of the fence on Chestnut
Street at Marshall Park for more safe parking, complete the dugouts and erect a scoreboard at Marshall Park Base-
ball Field, and erect a fence down the left field line and clear out the undergrowth at Lesure Field. The future of
Lunenburg's recreation includes more softball and little league fields, more lighted tennis courts, the possible erec-
tion of a hockey rink and a recreational center. These ideas at this time are strictly ideas, as extra cost to the tax-
payers under present circumstances, we feel is too much to ask. Lunenburg will eventually need a Recreational Di-
rector who can devote his full time to the Park Commission before we can accomplish these long range goals.
-7-

Town Clerk, Tax Collector, Treasurer
Of the changes Lunenburg will undergo in the next ten years, it is doubtful
that any will have a greater impact than the rapid shift in "Population Makeup"
which is already underway. Fully as significant as the dramatic drop in the birth
rate is the marked movement in the age

It hasbeen predicted that in the next decade more than half of the growth in
U.S. population will come in the 20-34 year-old group, the age bracket in which people start earning good income,
get married, set up housekeeping, start families and spend in a big way. The needs, preferences and habits of this
group will demand priority in all economic and social decisions made in the years ahead, and their life style, including
attitudes towards child-bearing, will exert the greatest influence on our community, as it will on society as a whole.
Therefore, in a community such as ours, creation of new households is forecast to accelerate for most of the next
decade - both from newly married couples and the growing number of single persons who prefer to maintain separate
residences. Even before the energy crisis, a preference among this age group for small cars and motorcycles washaving
a marked effect on the automobile industry, and this preference is sure to influence road-building patterns and traffic-
control procedures. More adult-type recreational facilities will also need to be developed. Elected officials will
have to give increasing weight to the viewpoint of these new voters.
The second fastest growing group in the next decade will be the 35-49 year-old group. By 1984 their numbers
are expected to increase 19%, a reversal of the pattern in the last 10 years, when the population between 35 and 49
dropped. These are the people who, climbing up the income ladder, move into bigger homes, become multi-car fam-
ilies, and in general upgrade living standards. This pattern of more expensive homes may well provide some measure
of relief for the average taxpayer. For example: in our Town today a $25,000 new home will engender an annual
property tax of $1, 170. If the family building the home has two children, the net cost to the Town for their schooling
will be $1,986, and it will cost approximately $335 for all other municipal services - a total of $2,321, or $1,151
more than will be received in taxes. This means that if, at present cost levels, 100 $25,000 homes are built, each
housing two school children, it will cost the existing taxpayer an estimated $40 more on his annual tax bill. On the
other hand, a $60,000 new home with two children will generate approximately $2,808 in taxes, and will create a
surplus of $487 over the $2,321 cost figure. Therefore, 100 $60,000 homes would reduce the tax bill of the existing
taxpayer by something more than $16 per year.
Whereas it is predicted that the number in the 50-64 year-old group, that with the highest average income,
will grow slowly and then diminish in numbers, the number of Americans 65 and older is forecast to rise 17% in 10
years. These people have finished raising their children and helped finance an additional generation of children, yet
they are still paying an average of 20-25% of their retirement income in local property taxes. Perhaps withmore num-
bers and a larger voice they will be able to spearhead a drive which would give them some relief from the property
tax which in many cases is driving them out of their homes, and stir the collective conscience of the rest of US to a
new recognition of their plight. The number of people under 20 is projected to drop 3% by 1984. This will mean
temporary drop-offs in school enrollment and could mean a break for taxpayers after years of rising education costs.

It is predicted that during the next decade the American way of life will be more limited in material ways
but perhaps more beneficial in tempo of living, human relationships and basic values. A resurgence of conservative
values could have a profound effect on the manner in which our Town grows. Few are willing to forecast what pol-
itical mixture will result from the mingling of conservatism with youthful emphasis on liberation, but it is evident
that confidence in all major institutions is down sharply, and that the new generation will no longer tolerate a
"Father Knows Best" attitude on the part of public officials, but will demand, as they should, to know the whys and
wherefores of the decisions that affect their daily lives.
-8-
===PAGE 11===
Planning Board
This past year a new member, James Mann, replaced William Mason who served his Town for ten years on the
Planning Board. Another member, Harold Harley, served his twenty-sixth year on the Board.
A Look at This Past Year's History
First, the March Town Meeting - Following much study and controversy, the Planning Board saw the Town vote
for a new stream setback and a new zoning map which introduced a new "watershed" zone, Residence B. The Board
also saw the Town Meeting reject the proposed increase in lot sizes and the elimination of two family housing, both
items which were designed to conserve natural resources and to stabilize on a long range basis both development and
taxes.
Subdivisions - This year the Board has been working on five subdivisions. One new subdivision, "Whalom Acres"
was approved. It is in Whalom and contains 15 lots, all of approximately 1/2 acre size. Application for one new sub-
division proposed by John Lampi, containing six lots on Reservoir Road, was dropped after submission of plans.
Another subdivision containing 198, ½ acre lots, called "Heritage Hill", is still in the process of being ap-
proved after submission in 1972. This subdivision is on West and Pleasant Streets on the old "Arnold" Farm. The
second section of "Broadmeadow subdivision off White Street is now being developed. It contains some 21, 1/2 acre
lots. The first section which is completed contains 9 lots.
The last subdivision that your Board has been working on is the "Whalom Estates" subdivision off West Street
and Hollis Road. This is an old subdivision which was started in 1961. Twenty-nine lots were developed at that
time. Since the lot sizes are in many cases less than 1/2 acre and inasmuch as the drainage problems in this area ap-
pear to pose problems for the Town, and especially for Whalom Lake, the Board rescinded this subdivision. There
were 156 additional lots planned for this area.
The Board was busier that ever with "Recorded Non-Subdivision Lots". Since revaluation of property taxes,
the Board has witnessed marked increase in these applications which indicate the breaking up of land parcels. These
lots are usually built upon if they receive sewage approval. In 1973, the Board processed seventy. In 1972, 44
were processed and in 1971, 34 were processed with 12 processed in 1970. Many of these land parcels constitute
subdivisions which do not have to comply with the Lunenburg subdivision rules and regulations, since they are de-
veloped on existing Town ways, therefore an inexpensive way for development using existing Town services.

The Board this year has concentrated its studies on subdivision rules and regulations, zoning by-laws and the
Scenic Roads Act. In the coming year, the Board expects an increase in new non-subdivision lots, especially in the
outlying areas. This unfortunately will lead to increased taxes, as historically a sprawl or spread out growth pattern
results in sharply rising taxes as opposed to growth concentrated around existing Town services core. The net result
here is that the developer delivers a lower cost/higher profit commodity, and the consumer, taxpayer, receives a
short range, low-cost commodity but a long range high-cost for added community services. With this in mind the
Board intends to further study land use, particularly in the outlying and watershed areas. It also will be working with
the Conservation Commission relative to wetlands and flood plain problems.

Planning Board in 1948, serving as Chairman of the Board from

and served in that capacity until May 1967 after which he con-

from Northeastern University in 1929 and was employed by
the Massachusetts Department of Public Works from whichhe
retired in 1972, after 43 years of service.

Tree Warden
Few roadside problems cause as much disappointment and frustration to mu-
nicipal and highway departments as Dutch Elm disease which has, up until now,
resisted control for more than a half a century. Carcasses of trees left in the
wake of the arboreal plague can be seen in urban and rural areas across the
nation - ugly monuments to one of nature's more irresistable forces. One of the
newest chemical deterents to Dutch Elm disease is Benlate fungicide made by the
DuPont Company. Basically, the objective is to prevent disease-causing micro-
scopic fungus from entering the sapstream of the elm. Once the fungus is estab-
lished there, tree tissues become plugged and the infected tree invariably dies.
The fungus moves from tree to tree in two ways - by elm bark beetle and
through root grafts that tie adjoining elms together. Over the years we placed
emphasis on methods of keeping the beetle population under control, and as a
result may have developed the mistaken notion it is a beetle-caused disease. An
individual beetle carries one million or more fungal spores that cause the disease.
More than a million beetles can emerge from dead elms left standing which
Richard W. Brown, Tree Warden
served as beetle brood-wood. Emerging from the wood, they begin feeding on
nearby healthy elms where those spores rub off their bodies and become established
in the new host. After feeding, the beetles seekout diseased, dead or weakened elm trees to serve as brood-wood.
A second brood of beetles emerges later in the season and the cycle is repeated. Fortunately, usually are more
resistant to infection by August. Timely removal of beetle brood-wood before the beetles emerge in the spring or
summer is vital to the success of any control program.
The second type of spread is through the root systems connecting diseased and healthy elms. The simplest and
most effective method in reducing losses is to mechanically sever the roots. Benlate (technical name Benomyl) po-
ssesses two important features. 1. It is systemic and moves internally through the tree. 2. It is active against the
DED fungus at very low concentrations without damaging the elm and with no toxicity to plant and animal life.
Infections from root grafts or late season beetle feeding do not respond to treatment preventively or thera-
peutically. Apparently the fungus in infected trees becomes too firmly established in the root system where it cannot
be reached by the chemical. We do not know whether Benlate will work against native bark beetle transmissions
because it has not been tested adequately against this possibility. New formulations of the chemical and other
methods of soil and trunk injections are in advanced stages of testing whether the success rate can be increased, and
whether the stubborn "root graft" and "carry-over" trees can be made to respond.
(Above are excerpts from article by Dr. Gayle L. Worf, Department of Plant Pathology, University of Wisconsin.)
During 1973, 217 diseased trees were cut down, chipped and buried. The Department of Natural Resources
gave three days of sampling elm trees amounting to $150.00, and six days of removal work amounting to $600.00.
Eighty-six trees were taggedand samples sent to the Shade Tree Laboratory. A total of $9,045.46 was spent under
the Dutch Elm account of which $555.34 was reimbursed by the County.
Seventeen Norway Maples and two Mountain Ash were purchased and planted on Northfield and Fairview
Roads. Trees will be purchased in the spring and planted on Leominster Road. A total of $5,756.30 was spent under
the Tree Warden account.

Cemetery Commission
During 1973 your Cemetery Commissioners and Superintendent met regularly throughout the year to consider
the routine business of the department. The maintenance of the Town cemeteries, the equipment, and the official
records have been carried out by Superintendent Porter. During 1973 the following major accomplishments were
made: the roads in the South Cemetery were oiled, and a tractor-mower was purchased. Most significant was the
installation of electric service for the North Cemetery building.
The Cemetery Commissioners invite all interested persons to the South Cemetery to view the new and perma-
nent bronze plaque placed at the grave of Samuel Page, the Town's first settler. This plaque was donated through
the generosity of many townspeople, and particularly the Lunenburg Historical Society who were also unceasing in
their efforts to see that our Town Patriarch's grave be permanently marked. On behalf of the Town of Lunenburg,
we thank you. The following table lists the names of persons buried Age in Lunenburg Cemeteries in 1973:

Library Trustees
One of the most important things to happen to the library this year was a start on revitalizing itsback storage
room for public use. This year the "Friends of the Library" group raised almost $700 through bake sales, craftsales,
book sales, a very successful rummage sale on the front lawn of the library, and through membership fees. The
"Friends" have decided to spend this money to make the former storage room into a room which will be available
for groups to meet in, for craft demonstrations and art exhibits and for the Children's Story Hour. This room can be
closed off by doors from the rest of the library so groups meeting there will not disturb others wishing to read. Since
there is no medium-sized free public meeting room in Lunenburg, it is hoped by the library staff and Trustees that
such a room will be useful to the Town.
Asummer reading program for children in Grades 3 through 6 was conducted in July and August. The program
started off with a showing of the film of the children's classic "The Secret Garden". Children were asked to try to
read at least fifteen books during the six weeks of the program. Thirty children read the required number of books
and many read a great many more. In all, the children read 690 books during the six weeks period. Upon comple-
tion of the program, the library held a party for the readers and one of the Trustees, Mrs. Janet Cragin, presented
each child with a paperback book. Mrs. Emily Foster was in charge of this summer reading program.
In the second year of running an adult film program on Sunday nights, the library receiveda grant of $825 from
the Massachusetts Council of the Arts & Humanities. This money was used to pay film rental costs which are not
covered in the annual budget. Twenty-four films were shown including a six film festival of Swedish movie direc-
tor, Ingmar Bergman's work. The third year of the film program started this fall. A Film Society made up of people
interested in helping the program survive both financially and spiritually was formed in November by Lunenburg resi-
dents who like coming to the movies at the library. Membership is open to all.
The pre-school Story Hour was so pupular last year that this year Emily Foster and Gloria O'Brien divided the
Story Hour into two sessions--one at 10:00 and one at 11:00 on Friday mornings. Coffee and doughnuts are served
in the reading room to the mothers while the Story Hour is in session.
In 1973, 23,821 children's books, 16,803 adult books, 1,030 magazines and 500 records were circulated. One
hundred and twenty-six inter-library loan requests were filled and 228 films were lent. The following people gave
books to the library: Dr. Thomas Mulholland, Mrs. Donald Cabot, Mrs. Gloria Karlson, Mr. Tozer, Mrs. Ruth
Proctor and the Lunenburg League of Women Voters. The Memorial Fund gave $50.00 for children's books which
was spent on a new set of Thornton Burgess books.
Library Trustees - left to right - Janet J. Cragin, Robert S. Tapply, Staff Member
Ruth M. Proctor, Chairman Carey D. Rhoten and Beverly A. Mulholland. Absent
when picture was taken were George R. Wallace 3rd and Beatrice M. Fay.

Personnel Committee
Each year brings new and different programs to the economic scene. From a relatively peaceful era we found
ourselves in a wage and price freeze and at the same time a period of rapidly increasing inflation. The Personnel
Committee has tried to keep the wages and salary schedule in linewith this inflation and still remain within the guide
lines set up by phase programs as established by the Federal Government. The Personnel Committee is again in the
process of negotiating with Local 353 of the International Brotherhood of Police Officers,
We note with sadness the death of our former clerk, Mrs. Doris L. Hartz.

Board of Health
The Town of Lunenburg is one of 16 towns which constitute the membership of the Nashoba Associated Boards
of Health, the office for which is located in Ayer. The other member Towns are Ashburnham, Ashby, Ayer, Berlin,
Bolton, Boxborough, Dunstable, Groton, Harvard, Lancaster, Littleton, Shirley, Townsend, Tyngsborough and West-
ford.
Regular meetings of Nashoba Associated Boards of Health are held each quarter. Each member Town has one
vote in the decisions of the Association. Through this organization each member town receives certain professional
health services in order that the requirements of the State Sanitary Code and the Regulations of each Board of Health
are met.
The following is a resume of the health services received during 1973:
Inspections of Food Handling Establishments
80
Visits to School Buildings
4
Inspections and Testing of Private Water Supplies
78
Housing Problems Investigated
2
Investigations of Nuisance Complaints
38
Sewerage Disposal Permits Issued (Repair & New)
85
Consultations, Visits & Inspections of Private Sewerage Disposal Systems
338
Conferences with Board of Health Members and State Officials by Nashoba Personnel
51
The number of children immunized in our schools and at the Well Child Clinics are as follows: Diphtheria Teta-
nus 147; Smallpox 70; Mumps 86; Rubella 65; Oral Polio 129; Tine Tests 128. The dental health program consisted
of inspection of 155 students in the 1st grade and 406 in the 2nd, 5th and 8th grades.
Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fitting
During the year 1973 two persons were summoned into
Court for violating the Massachusetts State Plumbing Code. In
one case the defendant was found guilty and fined $50.00 and
ordered by the Court to have a licensed plumber reinstall the
plumbing in question. At the request of the defendant in the
second case, a continuance was granted by the Court until Feb-
ruary 8, 1974. The Town was represented by Town Counsel,
Robert J. Larkin.
For clarification of homeowners of Lunenburg, the fol-
lowing is submitted from the Massachusetts State Plumbing Code:
The repair of leaks in a faucet, valve or other working part of
a plumbing fixture and the clearance of a stoppage is deemed a
minor repair for which no permit shall be required. This does not
include the alteration, replacement or relocation of any faucet,
valve, or other working part of a plumbing fixture or device,
water supply, sewer, drainage leader, soil, waste, vent or si-
milar piping or work affecting public health. For further clari-
fication of the Code, the Plumbing Inspector is available to an-
swer any questions. In 1973 there were 153 Plumbing and Gas
Permits issued. Forty-seven Plumbing Permits were issued for new
homes, 37 for alterations and 3 for commercial buildings. There
were 9 Gas Permits for new homes, 3 for commercial buildings
Thomas E. Mortis
and 54 for gas appliance installations.

Veterans' Services
The year began with an attempt to put this
department under the jurisdiction of the Depart-
ment of Human Resources and under a Public
Welfare system, which was a part of the new
plan that the Governor requested, but which was
defeated. We continued to provide services
to Veterans', their dependents, and parents as in
the past. Various laws were passed, and among
them was one that made this department avail-
able to the Veteran in assisting him to locate
suitable employment. This law is in conjunction
with the GI Bill of Rights. When we heard of
positions in labor, the information and data was
passed on to several veterans so that they could
apply for the position. We are honored to re-
port that three veterans were thus employed by
our efforts.
Another law passed adding to our work
load was the Supplemental Security Income Law
which was a Federal Grant of the Government for
low income recipients over 65 years of age, dis-
abled, or blind, and we assisted the Social Sec-
urity Department in this endeavor. We were also
to become designated as the Selective Service
Registrar for the area we assist, and all persons
reaching the age of 18 must make application,
and this can be done by calling 582-4785. The
Department moved into the Memorial Building in
Veterans' Agent, Alfred H. Gauthier
Lunenburg, and this office is open 9:00 A.M. to
5:00 P.M. except for Wednesday which is set
aside for Town visits. We now have larger office space which has provided more efficient service to our Veterans.
BENEFITS: The State Commissioner gave each and every recipient a 3.4% Cost of Living increase effective De-
cember 1973 which amounted to $6.00 on each budget. We hope to offset this when the 1974 income is computed,
as there will be an 11% increase in Social Security, plus a 10% increase in Veterans' Administration pensions. The
1973 Social Security increase did help in the ordinary benefits category but not in Medical and Hospitalization cate-
gories, as the costs in these spiraled. The State Department raised the monthly incomes for all veterans and their
dependents for medical assistance, so therefore we will have recipients on the rolls for medical only.
SERVICES: As in the past, this department was called upon to assist widows, veterans, and dependents to file their
annual questionnaires from the Veterans' Administration to establish their pensions for 1974. This department is al-
ways on call to assist all Veterans requesting assistance with regard to the Veterans' Administration or with the So-
cial Security Department.
This department wishes to thank all those who assisted in any way during 1973.

Conservation Commission & Town Forest Committee
Conservation Commission and Town Forest Committee - Seated left to right mg Barbara W. Brown, Harold D. Harley,
Chairman Nancy I. Sholl. Standing left to right - Philip E. Pearson, Douglas S. Fleming, Alfred C. Gilchrest,

"Thank you for showing the park to US. I like how the dam is made, and the best one of all was how those
beavers cut down trees. And Isaw one duck, and I learned about plants". This is one of the many letters the Con-
servation Commission received from third-graders who made an annual tour of the Cowdrey Nature Center tying in
their class work with the out-of-doors. Not only do the schools make good use of conservation areas in Lunenburg,
but also through our highly popular "Naturalist-at-Large" programs, conducted by Douglas Fleming, the adult popu-
lation is becoming familiar with and enjoying town-owned lands. Nine such programs have been heldwith an aver-
age attendance of 35, and one cross-country ski tour attracted 65 participants. These hikes were by snowshoe,
hiking boot, ski, or canoe -- depending upon the weather and the month.
Change is a part of any committee and this year we accepted with regret the resignations of Willis Woodruff
and Dorothy Millington from the Conservation Commission, and Richard Hayes from the Town Forest Committee, We
welcomed Philip Pearson and Aleck Solovei as members of the Conservation Commission, and Barbara Brown to the
Town Forest Committee. Aninitial start was made in an associate member program for these committees, and Dwight
Keeler and Stephen Willard have filled these spots.
Three hearings relative to the Inland Wetlands Act were held. The petitioners were William Lampi of Reser-
voir Road, The Massachusetts Department of Public Works relative to Route 2A, and William Proctor of Lancaster
Avenue. This Board also met with George Jepson, Regional Planner of Montachusett Regional Planning Commission,
to discuss open space planning and policy for the region.

===PAGE 23===
A 72 ₂ acre tract of land off Goodrich Street and Reservoir Road was acquired by the Town. The Harris Reser-
vation, as it is called, has a diversity of terrain and vegetation, and includes a two acre "kettle" pond.
A sincere thank you to the Lunenburg Grange for providing these committees with much needed aluminum
Wildlife Safety Zone signs; to Donald Gillis and the sixth grade students from the Middle School for the birdhouses
constructed for the Nature Center; and to the Lunenburg Exchange Club for the placement and erection of anti-
litter signs. We are indebted and grateful to the students from the Groton-Lowell Upward Bound Summer Program
who helped make new trails and cleared old ones in the Henry E. Cowdrey Nature Center; to Alfred Cook, Jon
Hangas, Steve Willard and Peter Stafford for developing a portage trail and canoe landing on Mulpus Brook; and
to Jon Hangas and Dwight Keeler for developing cross-country ski trails within the Cowdrey lot.
As 1973 comes to a close, we are face to face with the energy crisis. It points up very sharply that we must
have wise use of our natural resources. The spectre of limited natural resources has been introduced to the average
American, and he is beginning to realize that more of everything is not always attainable simply with technology
and money. We, indeed, MUST be stewards of this land and be vigilant in our concern for the quality of our com-
munity, the quality of the water, the quality of air, and ultimately the quality of life.
The Nashua River Program located in Fitchburg has engaged a firm for developing a Water Quality Manage-
ment Plan for the Nashua River Basin. Some of the major areas this plan will address include:
1. A description of Nashua River Basin, socio-economic factors effecting the basin and the role of the water
quality management plan in the basin.
2. The water resources of the basin such as, ponds, lakes, streams and reservoirs and how they interact with
one another.
3. A description of plant and animal life in the waters of the basin and how they are effected by water quality.
4. An overview of the existing water quality conditions.
5. The effect urban and agricultural development, terrestrial plant and animal life, and soil characteristics
have on the water quality of the basin.
6. Existing and planned municipal, industrial and institutional water pollution abatement facilities, and their
effects on water quality conditions.
7. Federal and State water quality laws, standards, criteria and goals.
8. Evaluation of water quality management alternatives for treatment facilities, non-point sources of pol-
lution, waste by-product disposal and/or reuse, and subsurface (septic tank) disposal techniques.
9. Recommended water quality management
facilities.
10. Water quality monitoring program.
11. Implementation of water quality manage-
ment plan through citizen participation and

Town Nurses
Nursing Staff - left to right - Doris M. Lahti, Shirley M. Carpenter and Gladys V. Barney.
The "Lunenburg Public Health Nursing Service" is part of the Board of Health. Three registered nurses are now
employed by the Town. Mrs. Doris M. Lahti, Mrs. Gladys V. Barney and Mrs. Shirley M. Carpenter who is em-
ployed on a part-time basis. Mrs. Marion L. Walsh is employed part-time to do the clerical work for this depart-
ment. All three nurses are involved in the Visiting Nursing Program and the School Nursing Program.
A total of 1, 116 Nursing Visits were made in 1973, classified as follows: Bedside Nursing, 801; Health Pro-
motion, 73; Visits to School Children, 129; Pre-School, 28; Infant & Premature Infants, 43; Pre-Natal and Post-
Natal, 36; and Tuberculosis Contact, 6. As of January 1974, the Nursing Fees will be increased to $9.50 per visit.
This will bring charges in line with other Visiting Nursing Organizations. However, no patient is refused service
because of inability to pay.
On April 12, 1973, the "Lunenburg Public Health Nursing Service" was evaluated. The Evaluation Commit-
tee consisted of Mrs. Ellen Kemp and Miss Arline Webster of the Professional Advisory Committee, Mr. William Benisch
of the Equal Opportunity Council, Mrs. Lahti and Mrs. Barney. The results of the evaluation reveals that the goals
of the agency were realistic and were being met in both the Visiting Nursing and School Health Programs. It was felt
that with the contracts from the Burbank Coordinated Home Health Care Program, The Home Health Aide Program,
Social Services and Supervision from Nashoba, townspeople were able to receive high quality home health care.
Lunenburg Public Health Nursing is a member of "The Assembly of Home Health Agencies" and "The Council of
Public Health Nursing Services". Both of these organizations have meetings to keep the Nursing Services abreast of
changing regulations for Medicare and Medical Assistance patients, as well as changes, in Federal and State regula-
tions governing certified Public Health Nursing Organizations. The above organizations put new bills before the leg-
islators to better home health services. All the nurses are kept up-to-date with the changing times by attending
in-service meetings at different area hospitals including Boston and Worcester. In November Mrs. Barney and Mrs.
Carpenter attended five meetings in Gardner on working with "Senior Citizens".
-22-
===PAGE 25===
The Nursing Service as well as the Schools (Report of School Health Program will be found in school section)
receive many valuable services from the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. In 1973, 9 Well Child Clinics were
supplied by Nashoba, with a total of 51 infants and pre-schoolers making 78 visits. Dr. Benjamin Bleckman, Pedia-
trician from Lowell, continued tobe attending physician. These clinics offer health supervision, physical examina-
tions and immunizations to parents of infants and pre-schoolers who do not have a regular physician or cannot afford
services of a private physician. There is no charge for these clinics. Any parent desiring information regarding
"Well Child Clinics" may call the Nurses' Office at 582-6702. The "Lunenburg Woman's Club" has been most kind
in supplying transportation to these clinics. The Nursing Services wish to thank the Professional Advisory Committee,
and members of the Board of Health for making our program a success.
250th Anniversary Committee
Lunenburg was incorporated as a Town on August 1, 1728 and will be 250 years old in 1978. Voters at the
Annual Town Meeting in March 1973 approved the naming of a committee to plan for an observance of the anniv-
ersary. A five member 250th Anniversary Committee was selected by the Board of Selectmen and has organized as
follows: Orrin J. Foss, Chairman; Jean E. Hobbs, Secretary; Robert J. Ebersole, Treasurer; Margaret P. Harley
and David L. Porter, members.
Preliminary meetings have been held for an exchange of ideas, and information has been gathered on what
other communities have done for similar observances. The Historical Society, with Mrs. Harley as coordinator,
has started to compile a history of Lunenburg to be published in booklet form.
Early in 1974, it is anticipated that major committees will be chosen to assist this committee, and a time-
table of events established. All Town organizations and all residents will be asked to join in the 250th Celebra-
tion. Persons with thoughts on what they would like included, or who would like to volunteer to help, are re-
quested to contact any of the above committee members. Active participation will insure the success of such an
important event, and everyone will have a chance to share in the fun.

Police Department
Many statistics are compiled at the end of
the year and are an indication of some of the ac-
CHIEF.
tivities of the Police Department. A total of 1263
complaints were issued against 875 individuals.
There were 238 motor vehicle accidents in which
four people were killed. Seven persons were ap-
prehended in the nighttime in the act of breaking
and entering into four different business establish-
ments. Through the courts, restitution to the Town
for damage to Town property amounted to $455.50;
and to private citizens, $448.94 for vandalism and
other criminal acts. Funds returned to the Town
treasury totalled $7,723 broken down as follows:
Administrative fees - $845; Court Fines - $3,165;
Paint Lining Machine reimbursement - $2,515;
Pistol Permits - $1,080; Firearm Identification
Cards - - $94; and other Firearm Related Licenses - -
$24. Federal Funds received were $41,231.32 of
Revenue Sharing Funds and $20,684.62 from the
Emergency Employment Act. Inaddition, $20,369
from State Motor Fuel Tax Funds can be credited to
the Police Department as reimbursement to the
Town.
Patrolman Michael A. Botallico, Jr. and
Patrolman Richard L. Lambert, Jr. completed a
ten week Municipal Police Training Course, as re-
Frederick B. Hobbs, Chief of Police
quired by law, with Patrolman Lambert ranking
first in a class of forty-three members. This now
gives Lunenburg an eight man full-time Police De-
partment, all of whom have completed their basic training. In December, Sergeant Roger A. Fitzgerald and Patrol-
man Botallico qualified to runa breathalyzer machine, and it is planned that all regular police officers will undergo
this specialized training.
A grant for $1,000 for the purchase of a breathalyzer has been awarded to the Town through the Governor's
Highway Safety Bureau, and the machine will be purchased early in 1974.
Lunenburg has a force of about forty reserve officers who are employed by private industry as the need arises.
The Town shouldalso recognize the services of the two remaining Civil Defense Auxiliary police officers who weekly
volunteer for church traffic duties, as they have for many years, at no compensation. They are Captain Edward L.
Worthley who was appointed in 1961, and Lieutenant Antonio L. Latini, who was appointed in 1951.
Again, it is recommended that more adequate quarters be provided to conduct police business. The quarters
presently being used are too small for efficient operation of this department.
The Police Department consists of the following personnel: Police Chief Frederick B. Hobbs, Sergeant Roger A.
Fitzgerald, Sergeant Hector J. Morin, Jr., Patrolman August N. Misner, Patrolman Alphonse J. Baron, Patrolman
Steven G. Whitney, Patrolman Michael A. Bottalico and Patrolman Richard L. Lambert, Jr.

Highway Department
Don't sell highways short in Energy Crisis!
Suddenly the unthinkable has happened and a
nation geared to the automobile and truck faces cur-
tailment in highway transportation. Officials and
engineers in the public highway agencies need to
keep their perspective in this time of emotional stress
over fuel shortages. The situation will tend to widen
the gap between urban and rural highway and trans-
portation planning, which will not be in the public
interest.
What ahead? For one thing you can count
on the continued wearing out of existing highways
from traffic and elements. Any slowdown in con-
struction, reconstruction and resurfacing that might
result from this crisis must be followed by a feverish
catch-up program. This was demonstrated following
World War II.
Waiting in the wings today are improved models
of highways and bridges that have taken decades to
evolve. Nothing shouldunduly delay the construc-
tion of such roads on a steady programmed basis, in
both urbanized andrural regions. It will take years
to catch up with the vast highway development job,
should it be allowed to get out of line because of
George G. Munyon, Jr. Highway Superintendent
current panic thinking.
(Excerpt from Editorial by Harold J. McKeever, Ru-
ral and Urban Roads)
The following is an explanation of services performed and projects accomplished by the Highway Department
on the Town's 76.97 miles of accepted roads:
Chapter 81 - The following roads were treated with 16,609 gallons of asphalt: Page, Flat Hill, Leominster, Oak,
Lincoln, Cross, Prospect, Elm, Hollis, West, Whiting, Stevens, Old Massachusetts Avenue, so-called, Boucher,
Cushing Lane and Francis Avenue, with 280 yards of sand used for cover. Pleasant View Avenue and Hillside Drive
were treated with 475.15 tons of bituminous concrete complete in place, and 489 feet of 12" pipe was used to in-
stall drainage in Oak Avenue, Leominster Road and Electric Avenue. Also, 139.35 tons of patch was used to repair
holes on accepted streets. Guard rail was installed, stone dust patching and gravelling various roads was done and
construction signs and small tools were purchased under this account. The total cost of maintenance of Chapter 81
roads was $26,937.86.
General Highway Maintenance - Drainage was installed in Cross Road, Highland Street, Old Massachusetts Avenue,
so-called, Hillside Drive, Highland Street and New West Townsend Road with 1501 yards of gravel used in this con-
nection. Also, 273.58 tons of patch was used on various roads and 33.20 tons of stone dust was used to fill in frost
heaves. Grass seed, fences and small tools were purchased from this account. The cost of performing this general
maintenance was $23,227.38.
Chapter 90 Construction - Bankings were graded and stock fence erected on Leominster Road at a cost of $14,566.17.
-26-
===PAGE 29===
Chapter 90 Improvement - A total of 1190.23 tons of bituminous concrete complete in place was laid on Lancaster
Avenue and Electric Avenue. Catch basins were installed in Electric Avenue and Summer Street, and guard rail was
repaired and painted on Chase, Shirley-Leominster, Lancaster, Electric, West Townsend, Summer and Fort Pond
Roads. A grand total of $24,692.18 was spent under this account.
Road Oil and Tar - -49,099 gallons of asphalt was applied on Cross, Kilburn, Old Massachusetts Avenue, so-called,
Rolling Acres, Page, Turkey Hill, Oak, Meadow Lane, Reservoir, Burrage, Maple Parkway, Pratt, Whiting, West,
Hollis, Youngs, Weatherbee, Kingman, East, Baker, Flat Hill, Holman, Chestnut, Pine, Arbor, New West Townsend,
Leominster, Laurel Lane and Howard Street. One thousand yards of sand was used for cover. A total of $19,013.26
was spent under this account.
Snow Removal - We used 779.505 tons less salt in 1973 than in 1972, evidence of our effort to complywith lessening
of roadside and waterway pollution. The cost of snow removal was $50,364.60.
Road Machinery Maintenance - The cost of maintenance and repairs to the Town's equipment, and purchase of gas,
oil, grease, oxygen, acetylene, tires, blades and chains was $26,556.96.
Town Barn Maintenance - Heat, light, telephone, stationery and postage, janitorial supplies and maintenance, of-

Special Accounts - In continuance of our drainage program, drainage was installed in Cross, Electric, Highland,
Hillside Drive, Burrage, Page, Hollis, Pleasant View, Summer, White and Whiting Streets at a cost of $29,268.96.
Street signs were repaired and new ones erected at a cost of $740.53. Brush cutting and ivy spraying on various roads
cost $1,481.14. The final section of Reservoir Road was reconstructed, and wood chips applied to bankings, at a
cost of $18,749.37. An International Pickup truck and a wing plow were purchased during the year. Materials
were purchased and engineering work done for construction to begin in the spring on Howard Street. Rubbish re-
moval was performed at a cost of $966.65.
Dog Officer
Dog has been "Man's Best Friend" for centuries. When man still
lived in caves and was clad in skins of wild beasts, the dog was his
friend. These men trained their dogs to hunt, carry heavy burdens and
to guard their personal property.
At present there are over 200 different domestic breeds of dogs
while wild breeds can still be found in Australia and in some European
countries. Dogs in addition to being pets are used for hunting and work-
ing such as a Shepard, Husky, Collie and Pincher.
Many dogs are mixtures of these standard breeds and are often
referred to as "mutts". It is often the "mutt" that disrupts otherwise
quiet neighborhoods with excessiv barking day and night and most often
on the rise and fall of the moon as our records of disturbance calls indi-
cate. These dogs are usually to blame for the vicious attacks on other
animals, damage to personal property, harassment of people on horse-
back and on bicycles and, of course, to pedestrians. The number of dog
bites to persons on bicycles increased this year, probably due to the fact
that the use of bicycles has become very popular.
August N. Misner, Dog Officer
It was very encouraging to note that residents in and about the
Center of Town restrained their dogs and thus minimized the problem
of dogs harassing school children during recess and going to and from school. The number of dog packs decreased
slightly in outlying areas as owners have become more concerned and have taken necessary precautions to prevent
dogs from gathering in packs. A more concerted effort to minimize nuisance complaints will be made in the Wha-
lom Area where a very heavy dog population is found. Therefore, with patience, discipline, and training and by
keeping a close vigil over our dogs, we can all show our neighbors that we have high regard for each other's rights,
and that we have not all "gone to the dogs".
-27-
===PAGE 30===
Fire Department
In 1973 the Fire Department responded to 293 calls for assistance as follows:
Service Calls 37; Brush Fires 29; Building Fires 21; Accidents 10; Mutual Aid 14;
Car Fires 17; Electrical 4; Furnace 2; Stove 1; Town Dump 4; Bomb Threat 2; Stand
By 2; Needless 4; Television 1; False Alarm 1; Chimney Fires 2; and Rescue Unit
runs 142. Of these calls, 94 were alarms.
For the fourth year our Rescue Unit has shown a large increase in activity.
In 1970 we made 55 runs, in 1971 there were 89, in 1972 there were 92 and in
1973 there were 142 Rescue Unit runs. New legislation which was passed this year
will increase the training time required for attendants and will have a change in
vehicle design and equipment to be carried.
The addition to the rear of the Fire Station being considered in 1974 should
satisfy our housing needs for an estimated twenty years. Our equipment is in ex-
cellent condition with the exception of our 1952 High Pressure Unit which should
be replaced in the next two years.
James P. Deming, Fire Chief
Department meetings were held twice a month with outside drills conducted
weather permitting. Six firefighters attended the Meadowood Fire School in Fitzwilliam, New Hampshire and three
members of the Rescue Squad completed the eighty-one hour Emergency Medical Technician Course and passed the
National Registry Examination. By 1976 the Emergency Medical Technician Course will be required for all Rescue
Squad members.

Inspector of Weights & Measures
Scales, gasoline pumps, fuel oil truck meters and other commercially used weighing and measuring devices
are regularly tested for accuracy once a year and unannounced spot checks are frequently made. Of the 101 weigh-
ing and measuring devices inspected in 1973, 98 were found to be correct and sealed. One pump and two scales
were condemned.
The Inspector of Weights and Measures worked with the State Division of Standards in obtaining information
for the Office of Oil and Gas of the Department of the Interior. Our Inspector also participated in a National Sur-
vey conducted by the U.S. Department of Commerce, National Bureau of Standards, on the local operations of the
Department of Weights and Measures.
In accordance with provisions of Section 41, Chapter 98 of the Massachusetts General Laws, all inhabitants
or persons having usual places of business within the Town using weighing devices for selling goods, wares, or mer-
chandise, for public weighing, or for hire or reward, are hereby notified to bring them in to be retested, adjusted
and sealed. The scales utilized by this department range from one gram to three hundred thousand pounds.
The Inspector of Weights and Measures extends his appreciation to the various establishments in the Town of
Lunenburg for the cooperation and assistance rendered.
Shown above is Wayne A. Balcom, Inspector of Weights & Measures

Council on Aging
The Council on Aging met regularly during 1973 at the Recreation Building at the Pearl Brook Apartments.
Mr. Howard O. Russell, who was chosen as Chairman when Mr. Arthur J. Brockelman became a member of the
Lunenburg Housing Authority, presided at these meetings.
Many members of our local Council attended area meetings in Auburn, Holden and Fitchburg. At these area
meetings many items were discussed with transportation being one of thebig issues with the elderly. Council members
wrote to their area legislators on House Bill 6070 which concerns all elderly citizens of Massachusetts. A very
enlightening evening was enjoyed when Mr. Bernard O'Neil came to Lunenburg and answered many questions on the
minds of our elderly. The subject of "meals for the elderly" is still a big issue.
The highlight of the year was the Christmas Buffet held on December 19th when seventy-seven senior citizens
were served a delicious buffet and enjoyed the entertainment provided by several groups. Mr. Russell was Master of
Ceremonies and the Co-Chairmen were Susie B. Logan and Kathleen M. Cooper ably assisted by other Council
members. Honored guests were Reverend and Mrs. Richard Dupee and Mary L. Padula. Hopefully the Christmas Buffet
will become an annual event, as many favorable comments have been received by Council members.
Council on Aging: left to right - Eino A. Liliova, Alfred C. Gilchrest, Chairman Howard O.
Russell, Kathleen M. Cooper, Arthur J. Brockelman and Mark G. Thatcher. Absent when pic-
ture was taken were Signe Schultz, Philip McNally, Susie B. Logan and Dorothy Millington.

Article 2. To accept the regular annual written reports of the Town Officers and Committees, or act anything thereon.
VOTED UNANIMOUSLY THAT THE REGULAR ANNUAL WRITTEN REPORTS OF THE TOWN OFFICERS AND COMMITTEES RE-
QUIRED BY LAW, BE ACCEPTED AS SUBMITTED WITHOUT FURTHER ACTION AT THIS TIME.
Article 3. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of
the Town of Lunenburg" by deleting the Classification and Salary Schedule voted on March 4, 1972 and substituting therefor the
following: Classification and Salary Schedule proposed by the Personnel Committee and dated March, 1973 effective from that
date until March, 1974; Classification and Salary Schedule proposed by the Personnel Committee and dated March, 1974, effective
from that date through June, 1974; by deleting from Section 9, ADJUSTMENT POLICIES, the first sentence, and substituting there-
for the following new sentence: "(a) Except provided in Section 11b, hereunder, and subject to the approval of the appropriate
Department Head and Personnel Committee, every employee subject to this plan who is in the continuous employment of the Town,
and who has a satisfactory performance record, shall be eligible for advancement as shown in the Salary Schedule until maximum
for the job is reached. "; by deleting from the first and second sentences of Section 12, GRIEVANCE PROCEDURE, the words
"immediate supervisor" and substituting therefor in both sentences the words "Department Head"; by deleting from the first sentence
of Section 17, PAID VACATIONS, the words "After completion of 10 years continuous service" and substituting therefor the words
"After completion of 5 years continuous service"; by adding at the end of paragraph 1, Section 17, PAID VACATIONS, the fol-
lowing: "Provided, however, that: 1. Persons first employed during the months of June or July of any year shall be credited with
10 days (2 calendar weeks) on the next succeeding June 1, and thereafter on each succeeding June 1, to 5 years of continuous
service. 2. Persons first employed during the months of August through May of any year shall on the next succeeding June 1 be
credited with one day for each full month of service completed, and thereafter on each succeeding June 1 to 5 years of continuous
service shall be credited with 10 days (2 calendar weeks). 3. On the next June 1 succeeding 5 years of continuous service and
thereafter on each succeeding June 1 to 15 years of continuous service, 15 days (3 calendar weeks) vacation credits shall be added.
4. On the next June 1 succeeding 15 years of continuous service and to retirement, 20 paid days (4 calendar weeks) vacation
credits shall be added. "; by deleting from the second sentence of paragraph 4, Section 18, PAID HOLIDAYS, the words "double
the hourly base rate in lieu of holiday time. ", and substituting therefor the words "one and one-half times the regular hourly rate
for all hours worked on the holiday in addition to his holiday pay "; and by adding in the second sentence of paragraph 5 of
Section 18, PAID HOLIDAYS, after the word "Saturday" the words "or an employee's day off ", or act anything thereon. (Ap-
proved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED.
-63-
===PAGE 66===
Article 4. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article II of its By-laws by adding after Section 8 the following Section
to be known as Section 9; "Fees collected for the Town by the Town Clerk and Tax Collector by virtue of these offices shall be
paid into the treasury.", or act anything thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 5. To see if the Townwill vote to increase the level of benefits for contributory group life and health insurance for
employees in the service of the Town and their dependents, or act anything thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee.)
VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO PASS OVER ARTICLE 5.
Article 6. To see if the Town will vote to pay, in addition to the payment of fifty per cent of a premium for contributory
group life and health insurance for employees in the service of the Town and their dependents, a subsidiary or additional rate of
twenty-five per cent, or act anything thereon. (Disapproved by the Finance Committee.) MOTION NOT CARRIED.
Article 7. To see if the Town will vote to accept Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 180, Section 17G, requiring the
Town to withhold an agency service fee for non-members of various associations of Town employees as a condition of continued
employment, or act anything thereon. MOTION NOT CARRIED.
Article 8. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate fromavailable funds, all sums
of money necessary to defray the charges and expenses of the Town for the current fiscal year and to fix for the fiscal year com-
mencing January 1, 1973 and ending June 30, 1974 the salaries and compensation of all elected officers of the Town and any
other items included in the budget of the Finance Committee, or act anything thereon. VOTED THAT THE BUDGET BE TAKEN
UP AT THIS TIME AND THAT THE TOWN RAISE BY TAXATIONAND APPROPRIATE THOSE SPECIFIC SUMS OF MONEY REC-
OMMENDED THEREINBY THE FINANCECOMMITTEE FOR THOSE PURPOSES DESIGNATED, THE SALARIES OF ALL ELECTED
OFFICIALS TO BE RETROACTIVE TO JANUARY 1, 1973, EXCEPTING THAT $50,000.00 OF THE TOTAL APPROPRIATED TO
THE POLICE DEPARTMENT BE APPROPRIATED FROM THE REVENUE SHARING ACCOUNT, AND THAT $20,000 BE APPROPRI-
ATED TO THE RESERVE FUND FROM THE OVERLAY SURPLUS ACCOUNT, AND THAT THE TOWN APPROVE THE APPROPRI-
ATION OF $2,782.13 FROM MONIES RECEIVED FROM THE STATE AS AID TO LIBRARIES AND $1,112.32 IN COUNTY DOG
REFUNDS TO SUPPLEMENT AMOUNTS APPROPRIATED UNDER THE BUDGET FOR LIBRARY PURPOSES.

Article 9. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum
of $9,150.00, or some other sum or sums, to defray the cost of a soil mapping survey of the Town, the maps to be drawn in cooper-
ation with the United States Soil Conservation Service, or act anything thereon. MOTION NOT CARRIED.
Article 10. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law of the Town of Lunenburg by amending Section
III-2A-2 by (a) striking out where it appears the word "By-law" and inserting in place thereof the words "this amendment"; (b)
by striking out words and numerals where they appear herein as follows: "20,000 square feet if in a Residence A District", "40,000
square feet if in a Residence Bor Outlying District", and inserting in place thereof the following: "60,000 square feet in area if
in Residence A, Residence B or Outlying District" or instead, by inserting the words and numerals as follows: "60,000 square feet
in area if in Residence A and 80,000 square feet in area if in Residence B or Outlying District", or act anything thereon. MO-
TION NOT CARRIED.
Article 11. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law of the Town of Lunenburg by revising the Protective
By-law Map of the Town of Lunenburg by delineating thereon as Residence A District those portions of the existing Residence A
and Residence B Districts which are not located in the Town's watershed area, all as shown on a revised map, and by delineating
as Residence B District those portions of the existing Residence A and Residence B Districts which are in the Town's watershed area,
all as shown onsaid revised map to be known as "Revised Protective By-law Map of the Town of Lunenburg, Massachusetts, revised
as of March 3, 1973" and to amend Section II-2 by deleting therefrom the title of the map and the date thereof and by inserting
in place thereof the following "Revised Protective By-law Map of the Town of Lunenburg, Massachusetts, revised March 3, 1973",
or act anything thereon. VOTED TO AMEND THE PROTECTIVE BY-LAW OF THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG BY REVISING THE
PROTECTIVEBY-LAW MAP OF THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG BY DELINEATING THEREONAS RESIDENCE A DISTRICT ALL OF
EXISTING RESIDENCE A DISTRICT AND THOSE PORTIONS OF THE EXISTING RESIDENCE B DISTRICT WHICH ARE NOT IN
THE TOWN'S WATERSHED AREA, AND BY DELINEATING AS RESIDENCE B DISTRICT THOSE PORTIONS OF THE EXISTING
RESIDENCE B AND OUTLYING DISTRICTS, WHICH ARE IN THE TOWN'S WATERSHED AREA, AND BY DELINEATING AS
OUTLYING DISTRICT THE REMAINING PORTION OF EXISTING OUTLYING DISTRICT, ALL AS SHOWN ON SAID REVISED
MAP TO BE KNOWN AS "REVISED PROTECTIVE BY-LAW MAP OF THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG, MASSACHUSETTS, DATED
MARCH 3, 1973", AND TO AMEND SECTION II-2BY DELETING THEREFROMTHE TITLE OF THE MAP AND THEDATE THEREOF
AND BY INSERTING IN PLACE THEREOF THE FOLLOWING: "REVISED PROTECTIVEBY-LAW MAP OF THE TOWN OF LUNEN-

Article 12. To see if the Town will vote to amend Section IV of the Protective By-law of the Town of Lunenburg, entitled
"Lot Size Regulations", so that the required lot size in Residence Bwill be 80,000 square feet and the required lot width through
the building will be 350 feet; and amend Section V-5 so that in Residence B the required side yard width will be 20 feet for a
detached accessory building if 150 feet or more from the exterior line of any street, otherwise feet; 50 feet for all other buildings;
and the required rear yard depth will be 20 feet for a detached accessory building; 60 feet for all other buildings, or act anything
thereon. MOTION NOT CARRIED.
Article 13. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law of the Town of Lunenburg by addingafter Section
V-10 a new Section to be known as Section V-11 as follows: "Section V-11, "Lot Monuments." No building permit shall be ap-
proved for a new dwelling unit until permanent monuments 3'6" in length have been installed at all corners of the lot. ", or act
anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY THAT THE PROTECTIVE BY-LAW OF THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG BE AMENDED
BY ADDING AFTER SECTION V-10 A NEW SECTION TO BE KNOWN AS SECTION V-11 AS FOLLOWS: "SECTION V-11,
"LOT MONUMENTS." NO BUILDING PERMIT SHALL BE APPROVED FOR A NEW DWELLING UNIT UNTIL EXISTING PERMA-
NENT OR NEW PERMANENT MONUMENTS A MINIMUM OF 30 INCHES IN LENGTH HAVE BEEN INSTALLED DEFINING THE
LOT.
Article 14. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law of the Town of Lunenburg by adding at the end of
Section V-4, the following sentence, "Any building permit granted for a street corner lot shall be conditional to the owner gratui-
tously granting to the Town an easement in said lot for the purpose of rounding or cutting back the corner thereof so as to provide
an outside street line radius of not less than 20 feet, said easement to be executed and delivered to the Town prior to commencement
of construction on said lot", or act anything thereon. MOTION NOT CARRIED.
Article 15. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law of the Town of Lunenburg by adding a new Section
to be known as Section V-12as follows: "Section V-12, "Stream Setback.' No part of a sewage disposal system for a new dwell-
ing unit shall be within 75 feet of a stream, pond or swamp if in Residence Aor within 100 feet if in B or Outlying District. or
act anything thereon. VOTED.

Article 20. To see if the Town will vote to amend its By-laws by deleting Section 2 of Article I, and inserting in place
thereof the following Section 2: "The Annual Town Meeting shall be held on the second Saturday of April of each year commencing
at 1:30 P.M. in the High School Auditoriumand all required reports shall be made at that time", or act anything thereon. VOTED
UNANIMOUSLY TO AMEND THE BY-LAWS BY DELETING SECTION 2 OF ARTICLE I, AND INSERTING INPLACE THEREOF
THE FOLLOWING SECTION 2: THE ANNUAL TOWN MEETING SHALLBEHELD ON THEFIRST SATURDAY OF APRIL OF EACH
YEAR COMMENCING AT 1:30 P.M. INTHE HIGH HOOLAUDITORIUMAND ALL REQUIRED REPORTS SHALL BE MADE AT
THAT TIME.
Article 21. To see if the Town will vote to amend its By-laws by adding under Article V the following new Section to be
known as Section 12: Prohibiting Unnecessary, Excessive or Unusual Noises: A. It shall be unlawful for any person or persons
occupying or having charge of any building or premises or any part thereof in the Town, other than that section of any establishment
licensed under Chapter 138 of the General Laws to cause or suffer or allow any unnecessary, loud, excessive or unusual noises in
the operation of any radio, phonograph or other mechanical sound-making device or instrument, or reproducing device or instrument,
or in the playing of any band, orchestra, musician or group of musicians, or in the use of any device to amplify the aforesaid, or
the making of loud outcries, exclamations or other loud or boisterous noises or loud and boisterous singing by any person or group
of persons or in the use of any device to amplify the aforesaid noise where the noise is plainly audible at a distance of one hundred
and fifty feet from the building, structure, vehicle or premises in which or from which it is produced. The fact that the noise is
plainly audible at a distance of one hundred and fifty feet from the vehicle or premises from which it originates shall constitute
prima facie evidence of a violation of this by-law. Any person violating this by-law shall be punished by a fine of not more than
fifty dollars for each offense. B. It shall be unlawful for any person or persons being present in or about any building, dwelling,
premises, shelter, boat or conveyance or any part thereof, other than that section of any establishment licensed under Chapter 138
of the General Laws, who shall cause or suffer or countenance any loud, unnecessary, excessive or unusual noises, including any
loud, unnecessary, excessive or unusual noises in the operation of any radio, phonograph or other mechanical sound-making device,
or instrument, or reproducing device or instrument, or in the playing of any band, orchestra, musician or group of musicians, or
the making of loud outcries, exclamations or other loud or boisterous noises or loud and boisterous singing by any person or group
of persons, or in the use of any device to amplify the aforesaid noise, where the aforesaid noise is plainly audible at distance of
one hundred and fifty feet from the dwelling, building, premises, shelter, boat or conveyance in which or from which it is produced.
The fact that the noise is plainly audible at distance of one hundred and fifty feet from the premises fromwhich it originates shall
constitute prima facie evidence of a violation of this by-law. Any person shall be deemed in violation of this by-law, who shall
make, or aid, or cause, or suffer, or countenance, or assist in the making of the aforesaid and described improper noise, disturbance,
breach of the peace or a diversion tending to a breach of the peace, and the presence of any person or persons in or about the
building, dwelling, premises, shelter, boat or conveyance or any part thereof during a violation of this by-law shall constitute
prima facie evidence that they are a countenancer to such violation. Any person violating this by-law shall be punished by a fine
of not more than fifty dollars for each offense, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO PASS OVER ARTICLE 21.

officer having similar duties, for the purpose of paying its police officers for such off-duty work details, notwithstanding the
the provisions of Section 53, or act anything thereon. VOTED.

No-School Signals - 1973-74
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after conferringwith the
Chairman of the School Committee and consulting with the Highway Superintendent and the Police to
determine the condition of the roads. The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme
weather conditions or unforeseen emergency; therefore, when schools are in session on stormy days,
parents are urged to exercise their personal judgement as to the wisdom of sending their children
to school.
The following schedule of NO-SCHOOL SIGNALS will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS of two (2) blasts on the horn at the Central Fire Station indicates no-
school at the following times for the groups indicated - 6:30 A. M., Grades 8-12;
7:15 A. M., Grades K-7; 10:00 A. M., PM Session of Kindergarten.
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of Radio Stations WEIM and WFGL, Fitch-
burg; WLMS, Leominster; as well as WTAG in Worcester, as promptly as the decision is made.
