Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
"What the wisest parent wants for his child the
community should want for all its children.
- John Dewey
It is a privilege to submit my Fourth Annual Report as
Superintendent of Lunenburg Public Schools. This is the 104th
such report preserved in the Town records and covers the year
ending December 31, 1973.
This year you will notice a change in our annual report
format, For the first time in many years, school principals,
Mr. Burgess, our Guidance Director, and Mr. Goodrich, my Admin-
istrative Assistant, were asked to write brief annual reports
WILLIAM R. HOLLAND
concerning their areas of responsibility. Since a great deal
Superintendent of Schools
of a school system's success can be contributed to the effec-
tiveness of its administrative team, it is important for the
BA, M ED., ED. D
townspeople to hear first hand from our principals and other
key school administrators. They are the individuals who make
things "go" in a school system and along with teachers help
determine the quality of the educational program.
With this introduction in mind, I will dispense with the annual comprehensive treatise from
the Superintendent of Schools, and instead, after a capsule review of the year, concentrate on one
or two happenings in Lunenburg Schools during 1973.
A CAPSULE REVIEW - LUNENBURG SCHOOLS, 1973;
Under new State legislation, the Lunenburg School Committee began 1973 with the complex task
of formulating an 18-month transitional School Budget (January 1973 to June 30,1974) as all towns
and cities in the Commonwealth moved from a calendar fiscal budget basis to a July 1st to June 30th
fiscal year. This conversion presented unique problems for the school department since costs for
one and two-thirds school years (e. g. two winters) tended to inflate budget totals. Also, because
of State laws, two additional months of teacher summer salaries had to be included in the 18-month
budget figures, a factor which increased the salary budget by an additional $210,000.
Because of these costs factors, the Lunenburg School Committee adopted austerity measures and
made significant budget cuts in the athletic, library, periodical, capital outlay, staff travel and
conference accounts, Nevertheless, a budget request of $3,569,306. was presented and approved by
the voters at the March Town Meeting, As in the recent past, fixed school costs in the areas of
negotiated teacher and non-teaching salaries, transportation, fuel and utilities accounts represen-
ted 90% of the budget appropriation--factors making it very difficult for the School Committee to
make substantial budget cuts by eliminating or curtailing other school expenses.
Also in March, Roger Raboin was elected as the new Chairman of the School Committee and Howard
Brock was re-elected to the committee for a fifth consecutive term. In June, Mrs. Marjorie Still-
man resigned as a school committee member in order to apply for the position of School Lunch Super-
visor. The School Committee and Selectmen in joint session then appointed Mrs. Jean Larkin, an ex-
school committee member, to Mrs. Stillman's position on the committee. At this writing, John Davis
has decided to seek re-election to a second term on the committee, however, Mrs. Larkin and Walter
Keeler, a past chairman and committee member for the last nine years, have decided not to seek re-
election. Both Mrs. Larkin and Mr. Keeler have given innumerable hours of outstanding and dedica-
ted service to the Town of Lunenburg and typify the fine quality of individual who has consistently
served on Lunenburg School Committees over the years. Mr. Keeler, in fact, has also served as the
-76-
===PAGE 79===
Area IV School Committee Chairman representing area school committees of the State level. The
objectivity, experience, and "savvy" of both Jean Larkin and Walter Keeler will be sorely missed.
In other developments, Lunenburg teachers negotiated a two-year salary contract calling for
an approximate 4% across-the-board increases in both the 1973-74 and 1974-75 school years. The
1973-74 salary range on the bachelor's level was $7940. to $12,307. in eleven steps and the 1974-75
range was $8240. to $12,772. in eleven steps. Non-teaching school personnel also negotiated two-
year contracts in order to place all school salary schedules in line with the end of the fiscal
year in June, 1975.
On the school level, 173 seniors graduated
from Lunenburg High School as the Class of 1973,
defying the odds, enjoyed a splendid day, weather-
wise, for its outside graduation program in the
football stadium.
Overall, school enrollment continued to
remain stable with an October 1st enrollment of
2200 students actually reflecting a decrease of 26
students over 1972 figures. The projected sharp
decline in elementary enrollment, however, did not
materialize as expected.
In the curriculum area, new programs in ele-
mentary math, social studies, health education and
science were introduced on a pilot basis with
great care given to establishing a thorough evalu-
ation of these new programs before expanding them
further. The most innovative curriculum effort
was the new Individualized Math Program (IMS), a
comprehensive mathematics curriculum for grades
1-6, which in lieu of textbooks utilizes 3900 lam-
GRADUATION - 1973
inated pages of carefully arranged skill
sheets, Being used in eight elementary class-
rooms, IMS is a highly diagnostic program and
allows students to proceed according to their
own learning rate. This new venture, prompted
by our need to replace outdated math texts and
our concern with pupil standardized test scores
in math during the past 5 to 7 years, has been
enthusiastically received by teachers and stu-
dents to date.
Also noteworthy in the elementary grades
are team-teaching arrangements found in grades
1, 4, and 6. Although each arrangement is
unique, clusters of teachers on these grade
levels have physically reorganized their class-
rooms, share facilities and equipment, flexibly
schedule and plan instructional activities to-
gether, and foster a more open, informal learn-
ing atmosphere.
In Special Education, Lunenburg Schools
OPEN CLASSROOM teacher, Mrs. McCue,
have been "tooling up" for the implementation
instructs Karin Anderson, Andrea
Mansour and Patty Lussier (L - R).
of a new State Law (Chapter 766) which now
directs schools to provide special additional
-77-
===PAGE 80===
services to children with special needs. The philosophy behind the new legislation is that the
local public school his the best potential to provide special help and training to physically and/
or mentally handicapped youngsters because it can best integrate the child with special needs into
his own family and with his social peers. The new law brings with it increased local responsibil-
ities and costs in the areas of screening and evaluation of all youngsters with special needs from
3 to 21. Further, special consultation costs for evaluation, costs for the development of new
programs locally, and costs for tutoring or for the placement of certain low-incident special stu-
dents (blind, deaf, etc,) in area private or state schools, are all to be borne now by the local
school budget.
At Lunenburg High School, outstanding student achievement again was dominant. Five students
were awarded National Merit Scholarship letters of commendation and a high percentage of seniors
qualified for alvanced college credit in the College Level Exam Program (CLEP) exams. The Lunen-
burg High School music program, with its rapidly developing feeder system in the elementary grades,
Not quite "76 Trombones" but just
as colorful and pleasant sounding
"ECSTACY OF WINNING"--Adele
--LUNENBURG'S MARCHING BAND AND
Harriman, Gail Okerman, Holly
COLOR GUARD.
Larkin (L - R)
demonstrated its excellence with several All-District and All-State music awards taken by Lunenburg
students. Also, the Lunenburg High Marching Band, showing steady improvement, was, without exag-
geration, the class of the Wachusett League.
In athletics, under the capable leadership of our Athletic Director, Dick Mulligan, Lunenburg
High is probably enjoying one of its finest sports programs ever. Coach DiGiammo's exciting foot-
ball team compiled the best record in Lunenburg's football history, the track team remained
undefeated, the baseball and field hockey teams challenged for league crowns, and at this writing,
the hockey team and boys' and girls' basketball teams are having outstanding seasons, The overall
balance and success of our athletic program is amazing considering the relatively small size of
our high school.
OCCUPATIONAL EDUCATION AND UPGRADING FACILITIES AT LUNENBURG HIGH SCHOOL:
Each year, major goals are set by the Superintendent and his management team. These goals
and objectives apply to the school system as a whole as well as to individual schools and depart-
-78-
===PAGE 81===
ments within the system. Principals and other key administrative personnel are held accountable
for specific objectives and are asked to submit written evaluations on goal progress at the end of
the school year. Many times specific goals might span one or two school years. One such goal
concerned updating High School facilities and providing for an expanded occupational education
program.
During this past year high school administrators and department chairmen were asked to under-
take a serious study of their space needs and reduce to writing their findings and recommendations.
From this data, I determined priorities and submitted a proposal to the School Committee for their
consideration. The proposal called for an expenditure of $39,513. over the next five years to
renovate classroom areas in science, industrial arts, the library, and in physical education. Also
included within the cost figures was the purchase of needed instructional equipment in these reno-
vated areas. All in all, four new classrooms would be created and equipped under this five-year
renovation plan with federal funds responsible for the creation of two of these new classrooms.
The big question asked by taxpayers is "why"? Why this capital expenditure at a time when
school budgets and property taxes are already spiraling upward at an accelerated pace? Although
it is difficult to summarize a 40-page proposal in a paragraph or two, a few of the major reasons
and determined needs for this renovation program are as follows:
(1) Because of our enrollment bulge, High School enrollment will increase by over 100 stu-
dents in the next five years. This projection is based on actual (current) enrollment
statistics. We are currently turning students away from the library and industrial
arts courses and are using hallways and storage closets in physical education classes.
This situation will definitely worsen in the future and may become critical if the
community should suddenly experience any new population growth.
(2) Because the High School is 18 years old, changes in curriculum and new State mandates
(e. g. new regulations concerning libraries, special education and physical education)
during the past several years have outstripped available facilities. For example, all
science courses now utilize lab areas, however, we have 19 class periods per week
which are now taught out of a lab setting. Because of the significant changes in
science instruction during the past two decades there is a desperate need to create
additional science stations and update our current science classrooms and equipment.
(3) The New England Association of Schools and Colleges granted Lunenburg High School a 10
year accreditation in 1969 but in its recommendations reflected serious concerns with
the lack of space and facilities in Science, Physical Education, Industrial Arts, and
the Library. If Lunenburg High is to continue to keep its accreditation status and
remain on par with the demands of public secondary education in the 1970's, 1980's and
beyond, it must act now to remediate these deficiencies in facilities.
(4) With the receipt of nearly $70,000. in federal funds for a Graphic Arts program, we
now have an opportunity to expand our Industrial Arts program and offer occupational
education courses in seven (7) major areas of industry--plastics, wood, metal, power
mechanics (small engines), technical drawing, electronics, and graphics. Coupled with
our developing Work/Study program (which now includes 40 students), business education
and home economics courses, tutoring and career development opportunities, and the
Middle School career awareness program, Lunenburg High School can really begin to call
itself a comprehensive high school meeting the needs of all its students and not just
the college-bound.
It is this last point that needs further elaboration. People become confused when they hear
that Lunenburg High School is offering occupational courses in graphic arts, metals, electronics,
plastics, etc. They feel that Monty Tech should be providing programs of this nature for our stu-
dents and fear that Lunenburg High may be duplicating programs already available at Monty Tech.
Further confusion exists when it is learned that Lunenburg is 30% below its enrollment quota at
Monty Tech. Why then, the taxpayer asks, must we offer occupational education courses at Lunenburg
High?
The answer is quite simple. Firstly, Lunenburg has a student quota of about 10% at Monty
Tech. This year, for example, our undergraduate quota as of October 1st was 94 students and we
sent (and were assessed for) 63 students. This is a problem area we are actively working on with
Monty Tech and hope to rectify in the near future. However, even if we were at full quota, we
would still have to provide for over 800 students at Lunenburg High School of which less than 50%
-79-
===PAGE 82===
go on to four or two-year colleges. For example, in the 1973 graduating class, 47% of the seniors
went to either four-year or :wo-year colleges, 6% went into nurses training or other post-secon-
dary training, and 47% went to work. This trend, which reflects a greater number of students
going to work rather than to college, is not unique to Lunenburg but reflects a general societal
pattern. What we are trying to do then by developing occupational education courses at Lunenburg
High School is to better prepare those 47% for the world of work. Presently, we feel we are doing
a credible job preparing students academically and don't deny the extreme importance of teaching
communication, computational, and other basic skills and attitudes; however, little has been done
in the past to expose students to a variety of occupations and to specific skill training on at
least a job-entry basis.
Secondly, occupational education at Lunenburg High School cannot be equated with the voca-
tional training at Monty Tech. The majority of students at Monty Tech spend one-half day for four
years in a superbly equipped shop learning their specific trade or skill. They get in-depth skill
training in a trade. Occupational education courses at Lunenburg High make no such claim. For
example, eventually, it is planned to cycle all 8th graders and some 9th graders through short
orientation courses in all areas of occupation education available at Lunenburg High. Hopefully,
this exploration will encourage many of these youngsters to transfer to Monty Tech for more exten-
sive schooling. As 10th and 11th graders, Lunenburg's non-college students will spend approxi-
mately 10 hours per week concentrating on two occupational areas and hopefully as seniors they
will have narrowed their choice down to one occupational area where they are concentrating their
study and skill development. We have a Career Education Committee which is currently developing
a curriculum for occupational education at Lunenburg High and it is realized that it will take a
few years before the High School occupational education curriculum is fully implemented. The
need, however, to begin the expansion, renovation and equipping of our Industrial Arts area is
therefore pressing. The receipt of our graphic arts grant could not have come at a more appropri-
ate time.
ELEMENTARY PRINCIPAL PLANS TO RETIRE:
In December, Mr. Richard Dustin, Principal of
the T. C. Passios Elementary School, submitted his
intent to retire effective August, 1974. After
twelve years in Lunenburg and thirty years in edu-
cation, Dick plans to return to the quiet of his
Maine homestead as well as planning to travel more
extensively. Dick Dustin's positive leadership
and contributions to the growth and current qual-
ity of the elementary school have indeed been out-
standing. Students, teachers, parents and his
many friends and colleagues will miss his counsel
and friendly manner. We wish him many happy years
of retirement.

REPORT OF ADMINISTRATIVE ASSISTANT
1973, from the perspective of the Administrative Assistant
to the Superintendent, provided a kalideoscope of happenings.
Happenings that ranged from routine to innovative, that spanned
grades K-12, and encompassed to varying degrees curriculum,
personnel, bidding and purchasing, pupil transportation, goal
developing, budgeting, Federal funding, teacher hiring, and
School Committee meetings, to name several. It being impossible
to report on each singular event, a sample of 1973's unique
blend is herein presented through the following highlights:
FEDERAL GOVERNMENT GRANTS:
BURTON E. GOODRICH, JR.
Administrative Assistant
Even though Federal Government grants for special programs
in education were more difficult to secure, Lunenburg was
BSE, ME, M+30
extremely fortunate in 1973 to receive funding for sixprograms.
The total amount of these programs was $91,569. and was dis-
pursed as follows:
(1) $15,780. was approved from Title I of Public Law 89-10 of the Elementary and Secondary
Education Act (ESEA), for providing the salaries of a Remedial Arithmetic teacher, a
Remedial Reading teacher, and half-time aide for Learning Disabilities. Receipt of the
grant marked Lunenburg's 8th year of participation in Title I programs.
Both remedial teachers worked with pupils in the elementary schools who were behind
their normal grade level of achievement in arithmetic and/or reading. The learning
disability aide worked with elementary school children whose visual and motor develop-
ment was sufficiently deficient to cause learning and/or socio-emotional difficulties.
By concentrating efforts on one child or in small groups of children, these specialists
attempted to ameliorate difficulties so that students could function in their regular
classes without continued special assistance. Evaluation of this Title I program
showed significant pupil gains in all areas.
(2) An amount of $500. was received as a Special Purpose Grant from Title II of Public Law
89-10 of the Elementary & Secondary Education Act. With these funds, the school speech
therapist was able to purchase special instructional kits of audio-visual materials,
test materials, and learning games for pupils with hearing and speech dysfunctions.
(3) $1873. was approved from Title III of Public Law 864 of the National Defense Education
Act (NDEA). These funds represented reimbursement to the Town of Lunenburg for 50% of
the cost of instructional equipment and materials purchased for use in individualized
instruction in science and math.
(4) With the start of the 1973-74 school year, Lunenburg expanded its learning disabilities
staff to include another half-time teacher to meet the identified needs of Middle
School pupils. Title VI-B of Public Law 89-313 provided $4320. for this teacher's
salary.
-81-
===PAGE 84===

of $13,450. and one in the amount of
$55,646. were received from Public
Law 90-576 for Occupational Educa-
tion for the purpose of developing
instruction in graphic arts. The
amount of $13,450. was tied into the

TEACHER HIRING:
Recruitment, screening, and hiring of
new teachers has been remarkably altered dur-
ing the past few years by a reduction in
teacher transiency and by the sheer number of
new applicants applying for available posi-
tions. 1973 saw this trend amplified in Lun-
enburg with well over 700 new requests for
teaching positions being received. Competition
for openings reached a new high in virtually
all areas except a few specialized and/or new
fields.
From the 700 applications received, nine
full-time and three part-time teachers were
hired. This represents an approximate hiring
-to-application ratio of one-to-sixty.
Screening applications and interviewing
candidates consumed considerable time of all
administrators. of all tasks, this perhaps
was the most important--for placing the best
possible teachers in classrooms provided the
nucleus for the system's continued success
and growth.

This past year saw continued involvement of the School Department's custodial/maintenance
staff in routine and preventative maintenance endeavors. Extensive painting, roofing, carpentry
and remodeling projects, which here-to-fore would have been contracted out,were undertaken by this
staff. The result was a substantial savings for the community.
Mr. Raymond Lemieux, Supervisor of Buildings & Grounds, and the total custodial/maintenance
staff are to be complimented for their efforts and the fine maintenance condition of all of Lunen-
burg's school buildings.
IN-SERVICE EDUCATION:
Through the collaborative efforts of Fitchburg State College and the twenty-one school systems
which comprise the Merrimack Education Center, it is possible to offer college courses that are
tailored to meet the specific needs of certain educators and/or school systems. An assessment is
made yearly of each school system in the M. E. C. consortium to determine which courses should be
offered and in which geographic location they should be held.
As part of Lunenburg's membership in the Merrimack Education Center, teachers and administra-
tors may participate in these graduate courses and receive local in-service or Fitchburg State
College credit. For the past three years, Lunenburg personnel have participated in several of
these different courses. During 1973, Lunenburg sponsored "Care and Prevention of Athletic
Injuries" which was taken by fourteen people and "Strategies for Learning Disabilities" which was
taken by eighteen people.
CONCLUSION:
The above mentioned highlights represent only a sample of 1973's happenings. Several other
events also contributed to the whole. In all, 1973 was mostly positive. It was a year of growth
year that served staff, community, and above all, students.
I wish to thank Dr. Holland, my fellow administrators and the many other persons who assisted
me in several of these areas. I especially thank Mr. Lemieux and Mrs. Stillman for the excellent
work they have done in their respective departments.
Respectfully submitted,
BURTON E. GOODRICH, JR., Adm. Ass't.

GRAPHIC COMMUNICATIONS TECHNOLOGY:
Probably the most significant happening within one year of curriculum development and change
at Lunenburg High School will be taking place as a result of the preparation, presentation, and
Federal and State approval of a Graphic Communications Technology project.
A total financial grant of nearly $70,000. to defray the cost of building renovation, purchase
of equipment and materials, and instructional reimbursements will bring about the implementation of
this project.
Its immediate objectives are:
a. To expand occupational offerings;
b. To provide exploratory experiences in graphic communications which presents ideas
to be communicated through drawings, words, symbols, and photographs;
C. To encourage students to remain in school with a wider range of training; and,
d. To provide students with more salable skills upon graduation.
Part of this grant has been funded and is being used in the input phase section of the pro-
ject. This phase which prepares materials for printing is under the direction of the Business Ed-
ucation Department and the equipment purchased consists of a composer, an off-set printing press
and related equipment.
Currently, the output phase, which carries out the actual finished printing of materials and
will be part of our Industrial Arts Department, is being planned through building renovation of
supply areas, development of curriculum and purchase of necessary equipment for implementation.
It is expected that a full Graphics Communication Technology program will be available to all
students in September, 1974.

COMPUTER SCHEDULING - MONTY TECH:
Although the school has been flexibly scheduled for several years with the use of commercial
data processing services, the availability of the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School
data processing services was utilized for the first time. These services have resulted in the
establishment of a student master file and the
production of student schedules after a pair-

homeroom and study hall rosters, and teacher
and room load reports.

that, for a first experience at this type of

also expect to expand and coordinate more uti-

instruction were made available to some of our

A core curriculum program has become very effective in an effort to make the adjustment for
some pupils to a new school environment more comfortable and acceptable. Behavior and attitude
patterns have considerably improved as a result of this program and will have great carry-over
value for the rest of the students' high school academic endeavors.
Several programs were initiated this year in order to familiarize parents and other citizens
with the school's curriculum. On two separate evenings, the entire staffs of the science and Eng-

the false impressions regarding high school youth today, this open house gave parents the opportun-
ity to observe young people in a realistic academic atmosphere.

REPORT OF GUIDANCE DIRECTOR
SPECIAL EDUCATION AND PUPIL
PERSONNEL SERVICES:
How can parents determine what a "good" school
system is? This question concerns all parents, and
especially faces those who have moved into new, un-
familiar communities. Besides looking at well-
equipped buildings, teacher training and experiences,
and evidence of administrative leadership, a parent
should look at the commitment of the community and
000
school committee to educating all of its students,
not just the ones who can function well in the tra-
ditional classroom. The degree that the community
is concerned about Its exceptional learners indicates
its commitment to all students.
There is no doubt that helping all students
JOHN 1. BURGESS
learn efficiently takes human and financial effort.
Director of Guidance
Additional staffing, teachers with more specialized
training in how children learn, additional supplies
BA, MA, CAGS
and equipment, and reasonable working space are
needed.
Lunenburg does make this effort to educate all its students, and its effort is demonstrated
visibly through the pupil personnel and special services it offers. Because of its commitment to
every child's right to a good education, Lunenburg has already provided many special services.
However, under new legislation (often referred to as Chapter 766) dealing with a wide range of
children with special needs, even Lunenburg must increase its commitment. We must identify and
provide services to those students who need more help than the regular classroom and teacher can
usually provide. Besides these students, we must now also serve those pre-school children who have
severe special educational needs, and those students who require special educational programs
through age 21.
What follows is a thumbnail sketch of these services and programs. We hope that you will feel
free to meet and talk with any of the special staff, if you feel your child can profit from their
programs and expertise.
SUPPORTIVE TUTORIAL PROGRAMS:
A. LEARNING DISABILITIES PROGRAM
Some students are just not ready or able to learn efficiently for their age and ability level.
Many of these students have problems in seeing and interpreting schoolwork and other visual mater-
ials. Some cannot really hear and distinguish verbal instructions from other kinds of "noise".
Others have not developed their muscle skills and coordination to the point where they can write
well, manipulate school materials, or even begin to coordinate their muscles for reading.
These types of learning problems are handled by the learning disabilities program. After a stu-
dent's particular learning problem is diagnosed,a sequence of activities is developed to improve on
the needed skills. Sometimes the student and classroom teacher learn to develop alternate ways to
get around the particular problem. Many high school students work under supervision with children
having the less severe problems, much to everyone's benefit.
B. ADJUSTMENT TEACHER
There are some students whose emotional needs seriously interfere with learning and developing
good academic skills. Because of this poor skill development and often inappropriate behavior they
become increasingly frustrated by school and frustrate the school in its efforts to teach them. For
such students it is necessary to intervene more deeply into their behavior patterns in order to help
-86-
===PAGE 89===
them and their families cope with and change the
destructive behavior. Since this involves a
large investment in time per student, the adjust-
ment teacher is only able to work directly with
a small number of children. However, because
the techniques of working with these children
can be used by all the staff, she acts as a con-
sultant to teachers who have children with
milder problems, and also helps develop other
small group experiences for some children.
C. SPEECH THERAPY
Since speech is the major way we communi-
cate with each other, speech patterns influence
how other people respond to us, and affect the
way we feel about ourselves.
Beginning speech, like beginning any other
complicated skill, is frequently incorrect. A
number of children do not outgrow their errors
by the time they are in the early elementary
grades. The speech therapist helps set up
classroom phonics and speech programs, but there
are many who need individual help. The thera-
pist uses various individual and small group
Mrs. Frances Bradstreet, LEARNING DISA-
techniques and materials to motivate the stu-
BILITIES teacher, works with a Middle
dent to hear himself and make the effort to cor-
School student.
rect himself. The therapist usually involves
the parents since most speech occurs at home and
parents must be aware of the part they play in
their child's speech.
SUPPORTIVE PERSONNEL:
A. GUIDANCE
There are guidance personnel at all levels of
the school system. Essentially, they help coordi-
nate, interpret, and initiate services for stu-
dents, teachers and parents. How and who they work
with most depends upon the developmental level of
the students served.
At the elementary level (Kindergarten - Grade
4), the counselor generally works most with the
significant adults (parent, teachers, specialists,
etc.) in the child's life to bring about change
more than trying directly to change the child be-
cause he is so little in control of his own life.
Middle School level children (Grades 5 - 7),
have much more control and responsibility over
their behavior. The counselor works with them in-
dividually, and in groups, as well as with the
significant adults in their life. Besides counsel-
ing and consulting duties, the counselor is actively
involved in the health education and career explor-
ation programs.
High School guidance personnel (Grades 8-12),
are involved with the many decisions that students
must make, and with the implications of such decis-
ions on the student's future. While parents and
teachers are still involved in these decisions, the

vice, and other agencies are used as resources by students and counselors.
B. SCHOOL PSYCHOLOGIST
The school psychologist works cooperatively with other specialists and teachers in helping to
evaluate and diagnose learning problems in school.
Since she also is a counselor at the high school, much of her evaluative work on younger
children is a result of requests from teachers, administrators, or parents.
In summary, Lunenburg Schools have special education and guidance programs in which the
community can take considerable pride.

REPORT OF MIDDLE SCHOOL PRINCIPAL
FLOYD F. BARNEY
ROBERT V. ANTONUCCI
Principal
Assistant Principal
BS, ME
BS, ME, M+15
The Turkey Hill Middle School became a reality in 1958 although we had to resort to double
sessions with the T. C. Passios Elementary School and share their facilities with them until March
of 1969.
The Middle School is comprised of grades 5, 6 and 7 and although our enrollment fluctuates
during the course of the year, the enrollment has stabilized around the 575 figure.
Since its inception, the Middle School has made some changes which we think have made school a
more enjoyable experience for the students and yet we have never lost sight of our main obligation,
"to meet the educational needs of each child to the best of our ability".
Middle School children in Lunenburg usually range in age from 10 to 12 years old. As all par-
ents and teachers know, this is a difficult age. They are no longer little children and not quite
adolescents. Because of this, we are constantly faced with the question, "how can we best serve
the educational needs of this age group?" Although the primary responsibility of a school is to
-88-
===PAGE 91===
teach the three R's, we also feel it is our duty to expose the students to as many facets of living
as possible. Hence, we have included in our curriculum many things that we hope will have a direct
influence on their social and personal lives and better equip them for adolescence and adulthood.
It is impossible in a limited report to go into great detail on every phase of our program,
but I would like to outline some of the things currently going on at the Middle School. Some of
the programs are new which we hope will be of benefit to our students.
DATA BANK AND SCIS:
Under the direction of Mr. Antonucci, the Assistant Principal, a new approach to the teaching
of Science and Social Studies was introduced this fall on the fifth and sixth grade level. Work-
shops were conducted by Mr. Antonucci and other staff members during the summer for teachers who
would be involved with the teaching of these new disciplines. Both of these subjects are now based
on an "inquiry approach" where a student is taught to investigate certain problems and make his own
conclusions. Instead of being "told" how something occurs the students learn by "doing". Elemen-
tary Science is no longer simply another reading lesson but now has a greater laboratory/activity
focus.
SCIS is an abbreviation for Science Curriculum Improvement Study which is an elementary
science program for Kindergarten through Grade 6. This program is currently being introduced in
our 5th and 6th grades and has been enthusiastically received by students and teachers.
Data Bank is a Social Studies program which again is for all elementary school grades. The
components of the kit, which are known as Data Banks, contain all the necessary material a student
needs for any given lesson. The fifth grade program is based around United States History while
Technology is the sixth grade theme.
OPEN CLASSROOM:
Under the direction of Mr. Bishop, Mrs. Crisci, and Mr. Therrien, a new approach to teaching
was piloted in grade six this year. It is called the open concept or team teaching approach. These
three teachers plus a student teacher were assigned 90 students and are responsible for all the
disciplines taught. The key to this team teaching approach is flexibility. They are not locked
into a bell schedule and can devote more time to a student, or a group of students whom they feel
need more help in any given subject.
These three teachers meet twice a week to
evaluate their students' progress and plan their
future lessons based on this evaluation.

REPORT OF ELEMENTARY SCHOOL PRINCIPAL
RICHARD H. DUSTIN
ROBERT FINNERON
Principal
Assistant Principal
BS, MA
BSE, ME
The 1973-74 school year opened with an enrollment of 804 at the Elementary School--identical
to that of the previous year. Predictions have indicated that elementary enrollments would
decrease but this has not materialized. In looking back six years when the public kindergarten
was opened in Lunenburg, the enrollment was 132. Our present kindergarten is 129. Class sizes are
above the desired average of twenty-five per class. With the exception of a transitional class at
the second, third and fourth grade level, which average 17 students apiece, our present average
size in other classrooms is: first grade, twenty-seven; second grade, twenty-nine; third grade,
twenty-seven; and fourth grade, twenty-nine.
In spite of class sizes we have done much in the curriculum area this past year and introduced
new programs. Some of the highlights in the curriculum area follows:
MATHEMATICS:
A pilot program in mathematics is being carried on this year in two second, three third, and
three fourth grade classes. The program is called IMS (Individualized Mathematics System). This
program provides maximum creativity and flexibility for teachers and pupils, and does not penalize
pupils if they are not proficient in reading. It covers ten topics: numeration, addition, sub-
traction, multiplication, division, fractions, mixed operations, money, time and measurement.
Within each topic, there is a carefully arranged sequence of skills to be learned. The skills are
organized into nine levels of difficulty, and each skill or "behavioral objective", is incorporated
into a separate skill folder. There are 376 skills in the IMS Curriculum. The skill folders pro-
vide for various types of learning styles, from manipulation of concrete materials to increasingly
difficult perceptual and abstract styles, Written directions, especially in the lower levels, are
minimal so pupils with limited reading ability can successfully use the materials.
To date, nearly all students and teachers have expressed enthusiasm for the program. The
program will be evaluated at the close of the school year to determine if it should be expanded
for the coming year.
SOCIAL STUDIES:
A new system of Social Studies called Databank was introduced into two fourth grade classes
this year. At this particular grade level the topic for study is "Inquiring About Culture". Two
areas of concern in social studies textbooks have always been "motivation" and "reading problems".
-91-
===PAGE 94===
To answer in part the concern of motivation, the
Databank system provides a wide selection of
teaching materials integrated into diverse teach-
ing strategies. First it recognizes that varied
resources by themselves stimulate the learner and
heightens interest in the classroom. Moreover, a
wider range of teaching materials make possible a
wider range of teaching strategies. With rich
resources at hand, teachers can vary their teach-
ing from role-playing to skill building, from
concept formation to problem solving, from simu-
lations to directed work in small groups, from
individual reading assignments to class
discussions involving value conflicts.
The Databank system accepts that reading is
important but places it in perspective, using it
as one important means, but not the only means by
which man inquires about man. Children do learn
to read the printed page but they also learn to
"read" pictures as well. They learn to "read"
behavior in their own classroom, in role-playing
situations, in simulations and games. Practice in
"reading" also includes experiences in interpret-
ing data from foldouts, cards, filmstrips, puzzles,
simulations and recordings. This variety of "read-
ing" experiences supports the slow learner using
varied materials; he is able to pick up and com-
municate clues which are less abstract and which
New INDIVIDUALIZED MATH STUDY materials
utilize more of his senses than does the printed
are being used by fourth graders, Ronald
page. It also offers a greater challenge to the
Goguen and Mark Rousseau (L - R), along

with Mrs. Rita Vignale.

To date, children and teachers alike have
shown great enthusiasm for the program and it
will be expanded into classes at the third
grade level next year.
SCIENCE:
The SCIS (Science Curriculum Improvement
Study) program is being tried this year at the
elementary level in some of the third grades.
This is an action-oriented and a materials-
centered science program. It consists of two
series of units--life science and physical
science which are related and sequential. Each
unit introduces important scientific concepts.
Discovery activities reinforce original con-
cepts and enlarge and refine their meanings.
Through his own investigations, the child's
thinking advances from fact to theory, from
concrete to abstract. An appealing variety of
of physical materials and living organisms
beckons the child to explore the naturalworld.
At the same time, the diversity of materials
and investigations enriches his vocabulary and
leads to growth of language skills.
Mr. Paul Romano and 3rd grade pupils,
Robert Denault, Jeff Wilson, and Kim
Shaw (L - R), approach the study of
HEALTH EDUCATION:
electricity with SCIS materials.
The Health Education Committee at the
Elementary School has been working on lesson
-92-
===PAGE 95===
plans in the areas of Nutrition, Mental and Social Health, Consumer and Environmental Health, and
Safety. These plans will formalize Health Education in the primary grades to the extent that a
basic curriculum will be offered to all children. It is understood that areas of "health" are dealt
with by classroom teachers in the course of Social Studies, Science and other lessons. Our purpose
is to lay a solid foundation on which the child may build a solid structure of health concepts and
positive self-image.
OTHER AREAS OF IMPROVEMENT:
In addition to curriculum, improvements have been made in other areas. A hot lunch program. was
introduced at the Elementary Annex in April of 1972. The food is prepared at the Turkey Hill Middle
School and transported to the Annex by our custodial staff. This past year a large warming oven was
Installed at the Annex and as a result we feel that our "hot lunch" program is just that. The meals
are served to the children hot from the oven, and participation in the program has been most reward-
ing.
In accordance with the goals established by the Lunenburg School Personnel, replacement of
old and worn furniture has begun. The Elementary Annex has received a "face-lifting" over the
past two years and we are now moving toward completion of the proposed renovations. One hundred
units of modern furniture have been purchased for the building. The colorful light-weight furni-
ture lends itself readily to varied and functional classroom arrangement as required by today's
concepts of individualized and group work. It adds more color and life to rooms which have been
brightened by our maintenance staff. This same furniture will be introduced over a period of time
into the Thomas C. Passios building. One room of furniture was requested this year and more re-
quested for next year.
Three part-time aides were hired this year for the curriculum areas. Two are working in the
individualized mathematics classes and one at the third and fourth grade level in the individual-
ized reading program. A part-time person was also added to the Learning Disabilities program.
New programs cost money, but we feel the programs started this year have been done in modera-
tion and after many hours of investigation and planning.
CONCLUSION:
I am retiring as Elementary School Principal at the close of my present contract year,
August 31, 1974. I wish to take this opportunity to thank the townspeople for their outstanding
support given me while assistant principal and principal. Twelve of my thirty years in the field
of education have been spent in Lunenburg. I have felt it a great privilege to serve in a town
which takes pride in its schools and gives to them its overwhelming support. During my tenure, /
have been blessed with an outstanding teaching staff and supporting personnel whose dedication has
resulted in continuous progress in the educational system. I trust that your confidence and
support will continue in the future.
Respectfully submitted,
RICHARD H. DUSTIN
Principal

===PAGE 97===
Retirements
MRS. LUCY W. CHARTRAND
effective June 30, 1973
Lucy joined the Cafeteria staff in September, 1957 as a General Worker in the Elementary
School Cafeteria. Effective September, 1960, she was appointed Baker at the same school. In Sep-
tember, 1962, Lucy transferred.to the High School cafeteria as Head Cook, a position she held until
March, 1969, when she transferred to the Turkey Hill Middle School Cafeteria as Cook/Manager. Upon
the resignation of Mrs. Phyllis Prouty in August, 1969, Mrs. Chartrand was appointed by the School
Committee as Supervisor of the Lunenburg School Lunch Program and held this position until her
retirement in June, 1973.
On Monday, June 11th, school personnel honored Lucy with a Farewell Party, which was held in
the Passios Elementary School Cafeteria. Burton Goodrich, Administrative Assistant to the Superin-
tendent, presented her with a pewter Revere Bowl containing the following inscription: "To Lucy
Chartrand from all her friends in appreciation for 16 years of dedicated service to the Lunenburg
School Lunch Program--1957 - 1973". Dr. William Holland, Supt. of Schools, presented her with a
money tree on behalf of the Lunenburg School System. Mr. Goodrich was in charge of arrangements,
assisted by Mrs. Virginia Welch, Secretary to the Supt. of Schools. Refreshments were handled by
Mrs. Genevieve Nass, Cook/Manager of the Elementary School Cafeteria.
In September, 1973, Mrs. Chartrand entered Montachusett Regional Vocational-Technical School
as a student in the Practical Nursing course, a field she has been interested in for many years.
All her friends wish her the best of luck in her endeavors.
ALBERT J. LEBLANC
effective November 1, 1973
Al joined the Custodial staff on May 22, 1963 as a Junior Cus-
todian in the Lunenburg Jr.-Sr. High School. Effective June 24, 1973,
he was appointed to the position of Fireman/Custodian and transferred
to the T. C. Passios Elementary School. He held this position until
his retirement on Thursday, November 1, 1973. In the School Committee
letter accepting his retirement announcement, "appreciation was ex-
pressed for Al's contributions to the Lunenburg Public Schools during
his 10½ years of service. His talent to "turn his hand" to a variety
of different tasks from fireman to locksmith was most appreciated by
all." A retirement party was held for Al in the Passios Elementary
School Cafeteria on October 29th, at which time a money tree was pre-
sented by Dr. William Holland, Supt. of Schools, and a clock radiopre-
sented by Burton Goodrich, Adm. Ass't., on behalf of the Lunenburg
School System. General arrangements were handled by Mr. Raymond Lem-
ieux, Supervisor of Bldgs. & Grounds, and refreshments were handledby
Mrs. Genevieve Nass, Cook/Manager, Elementary School Cafeteria.
MRS. SUSIE A. LOGAN
effective December 31, 1973
Susie, our School Census Taker, submitted her letter of resignation to the Lunenburg School
Committee on January 16, 1974, to become effective the last day of 1973, after completing 25 years
of service in this capacity. The School Committee accepted her resignation with very deep regret
and presented her a framed letter signed by all School Committee members, Supt. Dr. William R.
Holland, Burton E. Goodrich, his Administrative Assistant, and Virginia F. Welch, Secretary to the
Superintendent. According to the letter, "Future school years will not be complete without Susie
Logan taking our census. After the years of dedicated service in which you brought warm, friendly,
yearly greetings to townspeople, in addition to compiling census statistics, you should take con-
siderable pride, as do we, in a job superbly done." With her love of birds, the committee also
presented Susie with a ceramic chickadee, chosen especially for her by Mrs. Jean M. Larkin, a member
of the School Committee. School Committee Chairman Roger Raboin, Dr. Holland, Mr. Goodrich, and
Mrs. Welch, made the formal presentation to Susie at her apartment, on behalf of the Lunenburg
Public Schools. The letter further stated, "The School Census was Susie Logan, and inherent within
that statement is the genuine feeling of deep affection we, our school staff, and the community have
toward you.'
-95-
===PAGE 98===
DEATHS DURING 1973
of former school employees
JANUARY 19, 1973 - Miss RUTH EVELYN GOODRICH
Miss Goodrich graduated from Lunenburg High School on June 16, 1905, and from Fitchburg Normal
School in 1907. She began teaching in the Lunenburg School System at the old Center School in
September of 1931 as a teacher of Grade 1. In September, 1937, she was transferred to Grade 3.
Effective with the school year 1940-41, she was granted a leave-of-absence. She did not return
again as a full-time teacher but continued substitute teaching in the years that followed.
APRIL 27, 1973 - JOHN FRANCIS HAYNES
John began his employment on September 3, 1957 with the Lunenburg Public Schools as a Junior
Custodian on the night shift assigned to the Lunenburg Jr.-Sr. High School and Elementary School.
Effective April 1, 1961, he transferred to the Elementary Annex as a Day Custodian until his retire-
ment became effective on July 8, 1971. During his tenure with the Lunenburg Public Schools, he also
served as Attendance Officer from September 1967 until June 30, 1970, as well as serving as a
special police officer for the Town of Lunenburg.
MAY 13, 1973 - EMANUEL D'AMBROSIO
Mr. D'Ambrosio graduated from Salem State Teachers College in June, 1937 and came to Lunen-
burg in September, 1937 as a teacher of Business Subjects in Lunenburg High School. During his
tenure in Lunenburg, he also directed the School Band and served as Advisor to the School Yearbook.
In June, 1953, he resigned to accept the position of Director of Business Courses at Chelsea High
School.
JULY 9, 1973 - Mrs. JENNIE E. DARCH
Mrs. Darch was a graduate of Thayer Academy, Class of 1902. Prior to coming to Lunenburg, she
taught in Windsor, Mass., Westminster, and Fitchburg. In September, 1921, Mrs. Darch was appointed
to teach Grades 4, 5, and 6 at the old West School on Whalom Road. She taught in this school until
her retirement in June, 1954, after completing 33 years of service to the Town.

LeBLANC, Albert J.

Sr. Custodian, Elem.Sch. Donald Kenney (Transf.
from Elem. Annex eff.
11/1/73)
School Lunch Program Report
"You've entered the school lunch service at the worst time in its history." These were the
words oft times repeated by salesmen coming to my office. This only made me more determined to
meet the challenge head on.
School lunches to me are a very important part in a child's education. Where else can he par-
ticipate in so many new food experiences and yet be assured of a well-balanced meal. As you know,
we are required to serve a "Type A" lunch by the State.
Many changes have been made as we push forward to greater efficiency in service and to an in-
crease in participation.
Do you realize we have a huge baking program--rolls, breads, cookies, pastries, and that we
prepare our own pizza, lasagne, stews, etc. in our cafeterias?
Our menus, published weekly, will show you the variety of food in your school lunch program.
By preparing attractive, tasteful foods, we are endeavoring to educate your child to good food
habits which will become a natural thing in his daily living.
A financial report for the school year 1972-73 for the Lunenburg School Lunch Program follows.
Respectfully submitted,
MARJORIE H. STILLMAN
School Lunch Supervisor
-112-

===PAGE 116===
School Nursing Report
for 1973
The "Lunenburg Public Health Nursing Service" is part of the Board of Health. Three Registered
Nurses are employed--Mrs. Doris M. Lahti and Mrs. Gladys V. Barney, working full-time and Mrs.
Shirley M. Carpenter, working 5 hours per day when schools are in session. Mrs. Marion Walsh is
also employed part-time to do clerical work. All three nurses participate in a combined Visiting
Nursing and School program. Dr. John DeLeo is the School Physician.
A total of 129 visits were made to parents of school children for physical defects, absence
from school and follow-up on vision and hearing failures. The nurses also contact parents as much
as possible by phone to discuss problems students might be having.
In January, Marlene Lashua from "The Epilepsy Foundation", gave a very interesting and educa-
tional program to teachers and nurses. This program helped us all recognize the "Epileptic Child"
and suggested kinds of help that are available for such children.
Mr. Kerkos Kassabian from Northeastern University, gave a 10-week course at the High School for
"Care and Prevention of Athletic Injuries". Mrs. Barney participated in this course.
In the area of Continuing Education, all nurses attended the course, "School Health Program",
held in West Boylston. This course consisted of six, four hour sessions and proved extremely
valuable to our staff.
The Nashoba Associated Boards of Health offer the following programs in the schools--each pro-
gram requires signed permission from parents with the exception of the Educational Dental Program:
German Measles (Rubella) and Mumps
Vaccine to certain grades.
Grade I only I Trivalent Oral Polio
Series or Polio Booster for those
who have previously completed oral
series Tine testing.
Tetanus & Diphtheria Booster.
Grades 2, 5, 8 - Dental Fluoride Pro-
gram given by Dental Hygienist.
Grades ] & 4 - Educational Dental
Programs by Dental Hygienist.
Grade 11 - Tetanus and Diphtheria
Booster and Small-pox Re-vaccina -
tion.
We are fortunate in being able to offer these programs to our students; a factor which keeps
the Lunenburg Schools immunization rate at a high level.
Vision and Hearing Testing is done annually on all students, Kindergarten through Grade 12.
Notices of failures are mailed home to parents with a doctor's form attached. This form must be
returned to the Nurses' Office after the child has been seen by an Eye or Ear doctor in order for
the school to follow the doctor's recommendations.
The Nurses wish to thank the Administration and School Personnel for their help in making the
School Health Program so successful.
Respectfully submitted,
DORIS M. LAHTI, R. N.
Supervising Nurse

Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District
The Annual Report for 1972 indicated an enrollment of 889 secondary students, 148 post high school students in
health and other occupational fields, and 277 in Grades 9 through 12 on the waiting list. As of the October 1, 1973
census, we have enrolled 914 students in Grades 9 through 12, and 182 post high school students in health and other
occupational fields for a total of 1,096 day-school students. Wehave a number of former dropouts and high school
graduates seeking occupational development who are in available seats which are not occupied since we do not, as
yet, have a full Junior and Senior class. We would expect that our peak enrollment will occur at the next census
when an expected 200 will be enrolled in this school. (The original design of the building was for 1,000 day-
school students.)
Mr. Thomas F. Markham, Jr., was hired to replace the former Adult Education Coordinator and will assist in
special project services, including assisting in the coordination of district-wide projects. Again this year, repre-
sentatives from several of the local industries have made numerous requests for short-unit courses to update specific
skills, such as, numeric control for machine operation, fluid power control techniques, sophisticated welding meth-
ods, electronic mensuration and quality control methods, domestic and commercial heating and air conditioning serv-
ice certification programs, and a course to prepare adults for a Fireman Second Class State License. Also, evening
course offerings for adults have increased from six, which were offered in the Spring of 1972, to thirty-one courses
now in operation for the 1973 Fall term
hence, providing greater utilization of our plant facilities.
During the 1973 Spring and Fall terms, 1, 038 adults were prepared for gainful employment through our evening
occupational programs, representing an increase of 521 adults over the number enrolled during the 1972 Spring and
Fall
terms our first year of adult programs. In serving additional students on the secondary level, reach-out
and spin-off program development have received greater momentum as the result of the approval of a project propo-
sal and the action of our School Committee to implement the project proposal; therefore, giving more mechanical and
structural aid in enlarging the scope and effort of Montachusett Regional.
To date, twenty students have participated in Data Processing programs on an after-school basis. These are
students who are not enrolled at our school but are regular students from the sending schools in Fitchburg and Win-
chendon who participated in a support program to enhance their business education. Interaction programs com-
menced with Mount Wachusett Community College with the advent of a Career Mobility Program; i.e., students who
complete certain post-graduate health programs at Montachusett Regional may continue their education with ad-
vanced standing at Mount Wachusett and obtain a degree. Further studies are being made to developa contractural
relationship in cooperative use of facilities and student relationships. Numerous donations of equipment and sup-
plies have been received, including steel, an electric automobile, new automobile engines specifically designed
for learning purposes, mechanical equipment, industrial supplies, etc.
Our Dedication Ceremony was held on May 6, 1973, at which U.S. Senator Edward W. Brooke, U. S. Con-
gressman Robert F. Drinan, State Senator Robert A. Hall, State Representative Gerald P. Lombard, and members of
the State Board of Education lauded the school, its philosophy and methodology, as well as the graduation of over
500 students who have already benefited by successful entrance into careers and jobs.
Montachusett Regional's second graduation exercise washeld on two separate days since the auditoriumwas not
large enough to house both the post-graduate and secondary graduations at the same time. Our first class graduated
students who were transferred to our school from the phased-out Fitchburg Vocational School, and who came to this
school with advance standing. With very few exceptions, all the students were employed prior to their graduation.
An Open House was held on March 26 and 28 with over 4,000 people attending from all of the communities in our
District.
The Governor's Advisory Council on Vocational Technical Education selected Montachusett Regional as one of
two schools to evaluate for purposes of developing materials for its report as required by the State Plan for Vocational
-122-
===PAGE 125===
Education. At the "open hearing" the school was again lauded for its accomplishments with students, and it was in-
dicated that this school can set an example for its methodology, enthusiasm of staff and students, and accomplish-
ments of tasks necessary to prepare responsible and economically self-sufficient citizens for entrance into the com-
munities.
Last year, we had fifteen clubs. This year, our students have developed more extracurricular activities inclu-
ding an annual series of events such as a "Welcome Freshmen" which takes the form of a carnival. Our first football
game was won by our first football team! Other accomplishments include an annual Horse Show. This year's pro-
ceeds went toward paying for the electronic scoreboard and a summer camp scholarship for a deserving student. The
very successful Drama Club play "Flowers for Algernon", the Yearbook "The Odyssey", the school newspaper -
"The Growl", are all evidence of student identity and enthusiasm within the school. Since there are no study per-
iods in the school schedule, these activities are conducted during an activity period, as well as after school.
The Student Council, together with the Student Advisory Council, made several recommendations to the School
Committee which were discussed, accepted, and implemented including a smoking plan allowing for smoking only
if parental approval has beengiven, policing of the program through the students themselves, together with a Student-
Faculty Discipline Board. Among the of the students in their skill areas are the completion of a ran-
ger home at the Boy Scout Camp in Gardner, the repair to a bandstand in Ashby, the building of benches for the Fitch -
burg parks, the manufacturing of many specialty tools and parts for the Water and Highway Departments of the City of
Fitchburg, and other community services such as repair of vehicles and numerous printing services.
All new teachers have cipated in a summer workshop on individualize progression methodology. Teachers,
additionally, participated in a Project Decision Value Clarification Seminar which included a Drug Workshop. Fed-
eral monies received for project proposals amount to $567, 158 to date $126,000 of which has been received this
year. In addition, we have received 50% reimbursement on all operating costs as provided by Chapter 74 and 40%
for construction capital costs and interest.
At this writing, the administration is preparing the next annual budget. Every effort is being made to reduce
our per-student operating cost, in spite of the escalation in the purchasing index. While there was an attempt in the
legislature to reduce reimbursements to the cities and towns under Chapter 779, member towns are still entitled to
the 15% reimbursement under this chapter as members of a regional district. With Montachusett Regional's entitle-
ment of 50% reimbursement on all net operating expenses, and 100% reimbursement on student transportation under
Chapter 74, we hope to keep the assessment to the cities and towns at the minimum without impairing the objective
of preparation for educational competency, good citizenship, and gainful employment.
At the annual reorganization meeting of April 11, 1973, Mr. Morrill G. Sprague was unanimously elected as
Chairman of the School Committee. Mr. Herbert N. Hoffman is the elected representative from the Town of Lunen-
burg. Subcommittee appointments for 1973-74 were as follows:

Extension Service
This year, as in the past, Lunenburg youth have had 4-H Club work available to them. The active clubs are:
The Lunenburg Bridle Path 4-H Club, under the leadership of Mrs. Lois Camber and Mrs. Diane Moore; The Cookie
Crumblers, with Mrs. Agnes Price as leader; and Creative Mind Unlimited lead by Mrs. Dot Connell and Mrs. Ruth
Anderson. Town Committee Officers are: Town Chairman, Mrs. Ruth Anderson; Secretary, Mrs. Jean Stockwell;
and Treasurer, Mrs. Marcie Gauntlett. Trustees are Mr. Raymond Proctor and Mr. Richard Bishop.
The 4-H Cookie held in October was in charge of Mrs. Brenda Kimball. The top cookie sale camperships,
sponsored by the Town Committee, went to Andrea DeFazio, Patty Cutting and Cindy Smith. Also, individual club
camperships were awarded to a member from each of the clubs. This year a bus trip to the Eastern States Exposition
on 4-H Day and a trip to the Ice Follies in Boston were arranged by the Town Committee and enjoyed by all present.
It was with regret that we accepted the resignation of Mrs. Yvonne Bourque, a club leader and Town Committee
Secretary, who has given many years of service to 4-H work in Lunenburg.
In addition to cooking projects, The Cookie Crumblers Club completed other projects, such as sewing and
knitting. This year they proudly completed a Broomstick Lace Afghan for Christmas. Miss Heidi MacCracken, a
college student, was an observer and helper at several of the club meetings for which she will receive credits for
courses she is taking in school. The Club ended their year with a trip to Whalom Park and dinner at a nearby res-
taurant.
The Crafts Club have made a variety of different items. Liquid embroidery, decoupage, stenciling and cera-
mics were just a few. In this club we try to teach new and different things each year, and to do things that the
children are really interested in. A Splash Party and Cook-Out finished off the year.
The Horse Club members attended clinics at the University of Connecticut and in Bolton, Massachusetts. They
participated in the 4th of July Parade in Fitchburg and the Home Coming Parade held in Lunenburg. Ten club mem-
bers attended horse camp at Camp Marshall in Spencer. Two horse shows were held this year--a 4-H Show in June
and an Open Show in October.
A competitive trail ride for Horse Club members was held as was a trail ride for parents with judging being
done by club members. Mr. James Brooks held a longeing clinic at the Rod and Gun Club at which time he worked
with individual club members and their horses.
Many Horse Club members participated in Demonstration Night as did other club members. After giving their
demonstrations in Lunenburg, they competed in Oxford, on a County level, and in Bolton on a State level. Miss
Karen Allen was one of three from Worcester County chosen to represent US at the Eastern States Exposition in Spring-
field. In Springfield she competed against other 4-H members from all the New England States and represented
Lunenburg and Worcester County well. Congratulations Karen!
All in all the clubs have been busy this year, but there is always room for new members and for interested
parents for helpers and leaders.
